- **Verified**: unit tests for every mapping (1,147 pass), the merge rules, the writer round trip and the metadata documents; on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 (87 real-engine tests pass) one test exports a table with 13 columns of every kind, checks the exact file, validates it, refreshes after an `ALTER TABLE`, keeps a hand-set grain and a hand-typed column, imports a view, skips the tracking schema and a damaged file, reports a stale descriptor, and queries the source documents with SQL. **Not verified**: Fabric; tables with more than a few hundred columns or thousands of tables (each schema is read with one query).
- **Unlimited text stays unlimited** (follow-up to entry 36, at the owner's direction: the length is not the tool's call, and a project that wants to forbid `max` columns writes a unit test over the metadata). A bare `VARCHAR` is now a valid logical type meaning unlimited text: `nvarchar(max)` on SQL Server, `varchar(max)` on Fabric, `text` on PostgreSQL (`varchar(n)` to `text` is a widening change on PostgreSQL, so it plans without a question). `import-sources` maps `varchar(max)`, `nvarchar(max)`, `text`, `ntext` and an unconstrained PostgreSQL `varchar` to `VARCHAR` (exact) and `xml`, `json`, `jsonb` to `VARCHAR` (lossy: read as text); only types with no representation (geography, interval, arrays, user types) are left out (DDB-226). `define` proposes `VARCHAR` (high certainty) for a string column whose length is neither declared upstream nor written in the query, where it used to ask. A committed source type that means the same as the live one is kept as written (a refresh does not replace a hand-put `VARCHAR(500)` on unlimited text). Verified on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 (89 real-engine tests, 1,150 unit tests): a 6,000-character `text` source column passes through a model and a computed `body || '!'` into `nvarchar(max)`/`text` with nothing cut, and the tool then reports the table in sync. **Not covered**: SQL Server cannot index an `nvarchar(max)` key column, and the tool does not warn about it before `apply` (the same is true of a `VARCHAR(5000)`, which already mapped to `nvarchar(max)`); Fabric `varchar(max)` is unverified.

## 37. Project tests, layer 1: metadata rules (`dbdatabuild test`)

- **Design** is DESIGN.md 9.8 (layer 2, model data tests, is designed and not built; gating by test group is designed for and not built). The language is DuckDB SQL and the configuration YAML.
- **Rules**: `tests/metadata/<name>.sql`, one SELECT returning violations; `-- description:`, `-- severity: error|warning` and `-- tags:` in the leading comments (`TestRuleLoader`, strict about keys and values, with line numbers; prose comments are ignored). DuckDB parses a rule first (`json_serialize_sql`): exactly one SELECT (DDB-603); one it cannot run is DDB-602 whatever its severity; rows are DDB-601 (error, or warning severity for `severity: warning`).
- **Views** (`MetadataDatabase` in `DbDataBuild.Targets.DuckDb`, the one place besides `Sample` allowed to hold a DuckDB command, per `GateInvariantTests`): the project, source and model documents loaded into an in-memory DuckDB (external access off) and flattened into `metadata_current`, `metadata_columns` (same names and columns as the target's views, so a rule is portable), `metadata_models`, `metadata_sources`, `metadata_upstream`, `metadata_lineage`, `metadata_native_types`, `metadata_indexes`, `metadata_loads`, `metadata_hooks`, `metadata_index_advice`.
- **Command**: `test [names|files] [--tag t ...] [--limit n] [--strict]`, effect class offline only, a closed `data_test` schema (counts; per test name, file, description, severity, tags, status, violations, columns, rows, error). A new diagnostic family 6xx (601 to 603; the code pattern in `output.schema.json` and the catalog test now allow it). Registered in the TUI and the agent skill (a section on writing rules).
- **Verified**: unit tests (1,163 pass) for the file format, every view's content on a project with a source, an index and a hook, the views' columns equal to the published `metadata_columns`, pass/fail/warn/error and exit codes, `--strict`, selection by name, path and tag, refusal of DDL, several statements and file access, a damaged file not stopping the others, and nothing written. **Not verified**: on the real engines (the command never touches one, so nothing there changes).

## 38. Project tests, layer 2: model tests (`tests/models/*.yml`)

- **Format** (DESIGN.md 9.8): cases of `given` rows per table the model's query reads (sources or models, from their declared columns), `expect` (a list of rows, or `{rows, ordered}`) and/or `assert` (a SELECT over a table named `result`); `# description:`, `# severity:` and `# tags:` comments at the top (the SQL rules' header parser is shared, `TestHeader`). The YAML reader now records whether a scalar was quoted (`YamlScalar.Quoted`), so a plain `null` is NULL and `"null"` is text.
- **Runner** (`ModelTestRunner` in `DbDataBuild.Sample`, beside the other DuckDB code the gate invariant allows): in-memory DuckDB, external access off; the query runs as written into `result`; the output is cast to the declared columns and compared with `expect` by `EXCEPT ALL` both ways (so duplicates count), then positions if `ordered`; failing rows carry `_diff`. Bad input (an unread or unknown table or column, a missing NOT NULL value, a value that does not cast, an `assert` that is not one SELECT or does not run) is an error naming the cause, whatever the severity. The one SELECT runner is shared with the metadata rules (`DuckSelect`).
- **Command**: `test` runs both kinds (`--kind metadata|model`, names and files select either kind, `--tag` and severity apply to both); each case is an entry `<name>::<case>`; `data_test` now has `model` and `case`.
- **Verified**: unit tests (1,182 pass): the format (null handling, both forms of `expect`, 12 damaged files), pass/fail on rows, amounts (10 equals 10.00), duplicates, order and assertions, `_diff` rows, every kind of unrunnable case, a warning-severity file, a test for a missing model, a model that reads another model, selection by name, path, kind and tag, and nothing written. **Not verified**: anything on a real engine (these tests never touch one).

## 39. Lowering: ANY, ALL and row-value IN, and generated aliases

- **A correctness bug found first.** `x op ANY/ALL (subquery)` was *not* refused, as the open-items note said: it lowered, wrongly. DuckDB writes it as a MARK join whose comparison is a join condition; the lowerer read every non-equality condition of a MARK join as a correlation predicate and turned the mark into plain `EXISTS`, so with NULLs `x > ALL (S)` returned rows DuckDB excludes and `x > ANY (S)` as a value said FALSE where DuckDB says NULL (checked in DuckDB itself). Fixed, and the bug is why these now have differential tests with NULLs.
- **Built** (DESIGN.md 7.6): the mark stays symbolic and a filter writes it by what it needs: TRUE as `EXISTS (S WHERE p)`, FALSE as `NOT EXISTS (S WHERE p OR an operand IS NULL)`, following the filter's AND/OR/NOT; a mark used as a value is refused (DDB-324, "used as a value"). Row-value `IN` and `NOT IN` follow the same two forms, with DuckDB's rule that a NULL in any component makes the row unknown (the differential run found that SQL's AND would have disagreed with DuckDB). Subqueries with their own aggregate, window, DISTINCT or LIMIT are wrapped as a derived table. `=` ANY, `<>` ALL stay native `IN`/`NOT IN`.
- **Second bug found by the real-engine test**: a derived-table alias `s1` collided with a *table* called `s1`, so the outer column was captured by the inner alias. Every table and CTE name of the plan is now reserved before aliases (`s1`, `series`, `name_2`) are generated.
- **Verified**: 31 unit cases against DuckDB with seeded NULLs (ANY and ALL with every operator, empty and all-NULL subqueries, correlated, over an aggregate, with LIMIT and DISTINCT, under NOT, OR and AND, in HAVING, row-value IN and NOT IN), 6 refusals of value uses, the alias collisions, and on SQL Server and PostgreSQL 13 filters with NULLs on both sides equal to DuckDB's rows (1,215 unit and 90 real-engine tests pass). **Not verified**: the DuckDB 2.0 alpha, where the plans differ and 9 new tests fail (recorded in OPEN-ITEMS); Fabric.

## 40. Engine-difference probes, and the bugs they found

- **The probe** (`EngineProbe`, `EngineDifferenceProbes`, docs/research/engine-differences): about 200 scalar expressions and aggregates, each run on DuckDB as written and on SQL Server and PostgreSQL as the tool would send them (lowered, target-ruled, transpiled), over rows built for the edges, with the profile's collations on the text column. A case equal to DuckDB's is a regression test; a difference must be listed in `Known` with its reason. 1,244 unit tests and 93 real-engine tests pass.
- **Bugs it found, fixed**: any query with an empty string `''` crashed `render` (the plan drops an empty string's value); `string_agg` lost its separator and ORDER BY (`string_agg(x, ' | ')` became `string_agg(x)`), and other aggregates with data outside their arguments (`median`, `quantile_cont`, `first(x ORDER BY ..)`) lost it too, now refused; `position(a IN b)` had its arguments swapped on both engines; `CAST(decimal AS INTEGER)` truncated on SQL Server (DuckDB rounds) and `CAST(double AS INTEGER)` too (DuckDB rounds half to even); `x % 0` raised an error; `//`, one-argument `round`, two-argument `substr`, `year()`/`month()`/`day()`/`hour()`/..., `dayofweek()`, `date - date` and `date_part('dow')` did not translate or depended on `@@DATEFIRST`. Any lowering exception that is not a refusal is now a refusal, not a crash.
- **Written down, as matrix rows** (36 rows now, the matrix hash changed and the goldens were regenerated): `str.case_mapping`, `fn.pad`, `fn.contains`, `fn.mode`, `fn.corr`, `fn.string_agg` (PostgreSQL unsupported: polyglot writes `LISTAGG`), `fn.date_part_calendar`, `fn.substring_negative`, plus notes on `fn.length` (non-BMP) and `fn.date_diff`. The linter now also matches aggregate functions (`aggregate_function` nodes) by name.
- **Not covered**: the probes use the DuckDB plan of 1.5.x; Fabric; window functions with frames; the text forms of casts are documented, not fixed.

## 41. Source indexes and foreign keys

- **Exported by `import-sources`** (follow-up to entries 36 and 38): `indexes:` and `foreign_keys:` in the descriptor (`schemas/source.schema.json`, the loader checks that the columns exist, that names are unique and that a foreign key's column counts agree). Indexes come from the catalog shape already read for planning (`ObjectShape.Physical`, parsed back with `IndexText.Parse`); foreign keys are a new read-only catalog query per engine (`SourceCatalogReader.ForeignKeysAsync`). The primary key's own index is the grain and is skipped; an expression index, and an index or foreign key that names a column with no logical type, are left out with a note in the output (`notes`). The live table wins; added, removed and changed indexes and foreign keys are differences (`indexadded`, `foreignkeychanged`, ...), so `--check` finds them. Index names are the table's own, not held to the identifier pattern models use.
- **Metadata**: the source document carries `indexes` and `foreign_keys` (`dbdatabuild.source/1`, closed schema updated) and is published with them; two DuckDB views for rules, `metadata_source_indexes` and `metadata_source_foreign_keys`. Collation stays out (a native collation name has no honest logical name).
- **Verified**: loader and schema agree on 10 new cases; the exporter's parsing, skipping and notes, the written text and its round trip, and the six kinds of difference (unit tests, 1,255 pass); on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 the real-engine test creates a covering index, a unique index and a foreign key, checks the exact file, sees a new index as a difference after a table change, keeps them through a refresh, and finds them in the published document. **Not verified**: Fabric; descending keys (the direction is not exported); filtered or partial indexes (the filter is not exported, so such an index looks like a full one).

## 42. Dependency graph, model selectors and `graph`

- **Built** (DESIGN.md 9.9): `DependencyGraph` (names only: ancestors and descendants with distance and depth limit, levels, unknown tables; cycles end the walk), the selector grammar (`+x`, `x+`, `N+x`, `x+N`, `@x`, `kind:`, `target:`, `path:`, `changed:<git ref>`, comma intersection, `exclude:`) in `ProjectContext.Select`, so every command that takes models has it, `GitInfo.ChangedFiles` (working tree against a ref plus untracked files; a changed source selects its readers, a changed `dbdatabuild.yml` every model), and `dbdatabuild graph` (leveled list, `--columns`, `--column model.column` with both directions and distances, `--diagram dot|mermaid`, a closed `data_graph` schema). The command-line parser's response-file feature is off, because `@model` is a selector. The view `metadata_ancestors` for rules.
- **Behavior notes**: a term that selects nothing is a usage error (a typo must not run nothing), except `changed:`. A selector that reaches more models reaches them for every purpose (`plan +marts.big` plans what it reads too).
- **Verified**: 41 new unit tests (1,296 pass): the pure graph, the grammar, 15 selector shapes end to end, the selects-nothing errors, `changed:` in a real git repository (a changed model, `+` downstream, an untracked model, a changed source, a changed config), column edges and lineage in both directions, diagrams, a project with errors, and the ancestors view. **Not covered**: `changed:` ignores hook scripts and rendered files (only model files, sources and the config); a model whose only change is in a hook script is not "changed".

## 43. Table diff (`dbdatabuild diff`), version 1

- **Built** (DESIGN.md 9.10): two tables or views of one target, read-only, on the read login. Schema pairing (columns on one side only, types that differ, columns not compared and why), a key (`--key`, or the table's grain or unique key), row counts and non-NULL counts, repeated keys, and one `FULL OUTER JOIN` that counts rows only on the left, only on the right, matched and differing, in total and per column (NULL equals NULL; a presence marker so a NULL key is not an absent row; collation conflicts resolved in the left column's). **No value is read by default**; `--show-values --limit n` reads min and max per column and sample rows of each kind of difference, and prints them to the operator who asked, in the text or the `samples` of the JSON document, never to the statement log or a plan. Principle 8 is reworded to what it was always for: an agent can operate without access to data (the defaults hold none); showing data to an operator who asks was never forbidden. Exit code 0 only when identical.
- **Verified**: 12 unit tests for pairing, key refusals, the generated SQL in both dialects (text, `TOP` and `LIMIT`, collation, ordering) and that every query passes the read guard; the usage refusals before any connection; on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 a real-engine test with a wider decimal, an extra column, a missing row, an added row, a NULL against a value and a changed value: counts exact, nothing of the data in the default output, the right samples with `--show-values`, narrowing by columns, an identical copy (exit 0), a repeated key (rows not compared), a column of another kind not compared, a missing table (usage error). **Not built**: across targets, a floating-point tolerance.

## 44. Seeds, `seed`, project templates and `new`

- **Built** (DESIGN.md 15.6, 15.7): `seeds/<schema>/<table>.sql` per source (DuckDB, variables `seed` and `scale`, macros in `seeds/macros.sql`), `SeedLoader`/`SeedRun` (dependency order by table-name mention, cycles refused), `seed` command (`.dbdatabuild/seed.duckdb`), `sample` uses seeds and takes `--scale`. `templates/` embedded as resources; `new` lists and copies them. Templates `starter` and `retail`.
- **Lowering and matrix**: `SIGN`, `TRUNC` and `str_position` are now covered (spike cases `sign_fn`, `trunc_fn`, `strpos_fn`), which changed the matrix hash: goldens regenerated. Templates avoid constructs with no target form: whole-number sums are cast back to BIGINT (DuckDB widens them to HUGEINT), `//` is written as floor of a DOUBLE division.
- **Verified**: 1,311 unit tests pass, including one that takes each template through `new`, `validate` (0 warnings), `seed`, `sample` of every mart, `test` (retail 10/10) and `render` for SQL Server. Not verified: the templates on a real SQL Server or PostgreSQL (that needs the loading step, next), Fabric never.
- **Left**: Chinook and AdventureWorks templates (backlog).

## 45. `load-seeds`, templates on real engines, and three lowering bugs they found

- **Built** (DESIGN.md 15.7): `load-seeds` creates the seeded source tables on SQL Server or PostgreSQL and fills them (`SeededData` in Sample reads batches from an in-memory DuckDB; `LoadSeedsCommand` builds the statements; `GateStatement.BulkInsert` keeps the values out of the statement log). Preview by default, `--apply` runs, `--replace` drops first, otherwise it stops at an existing table.
- **Verified on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17**: `TemplateConformanceTests` runs both templates through `new`, `load-seeds --apply`, `render --write`, `init --apply`, `plan`, `apply`, then compares every mart row by row with DuckDB's result on the same seeds: 4 of 4 pass. 
- **Bugs this found (all silent wrong results, all fixed with unit tests that compare the lowered text's rows with DuckDB)**: a three-way `UNION` lost its third query; a one-sided predicate of a join's `ON` clause (`AND b.f IS NOT NULL`) was dropped; `CAST(double AS DECIMAL)` rounds differently on both engines (rule `double-to-decimal`, probe README). Fabric has still never run.
- **Left**: the unit tests for the lowering fixes use the small tables of `PlanLowererTests`; the retail template could get a seed with NULL-heavy data to exercise more join shapes.

## 46. Chinook and AdventureWorks templates, and what building them found

- **Built**: `templates/chinook` (11 sources, 11 staging views, 11 marts, 5 metadata rules, 5 model tests) and `templates/adventureworks` (23 sources, 23 staging views, 18 marts, 6 metadata rules, 11 model tests, seeds with macros for the dates and the price steps). Both are run through `new`, `validate`, `seed`, `sample`, `test` and `render` by `TemplateTests` and through `load-seeds`, `plan`, `apply` and a row-by-row comparison with DuckDB on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 by `TemplateConformanceTests` (8 of 8). `docs/research/template-findings.md` lists every problem they hit and what became of it.
- **Fixed in the tool** (each with unit tests, and probes against both engines where an engine is involved): sources load in a project with no `models/` directory; `a // b` parses in authored queries (`SqlParseHints.ForParser`); recursive queries lower (`LOGICAL_RECURSIVE_CTE`); constants carry their type; a window `count(*)`; a lateral series says why it is refused; target rules `pad-to-length`, `split-part`, `string-agg-array`, `date-plus-days`, `concat-plus`, `date-diff-boundaries`, `date-diff-weeks`.
- **Cataloged**: matrix rows `fn.split_part`, `syntax.recursive_cte`, `fn.greatest_least`, `type.decimal_product_wide`, covered window functions; refused and listed: `median`, `quantile_cont`, `nth_value`, `split_part` on SQL Server, a series per row.
- **Verified**: 1,372 unit tests pass; the 36 new window probes, the pad, split, date and concatenation probes agree with DuckDB on both engines or are listed with their reason; every mart of the four templates equals DuckDB's rows on both engines. Fabric has still never run.
- **Left**: the refused constructs above; `OPTION (MAXRECURSION)` for deeper hierarchies on SQL Server (it cannot go in a view).

## 47. Language areas, and turning the rewrites off

- **Built** (DESIGN.md 7.6.2): `rewrites:` in `dbdatabuild.yml` and in a model (`fidelity: exact | native`, `disable`, `enable`), `RewriteCatalog` (seventeen named rewrites, each with what it does, where it is required and what the engine does without it), `RewritePolicy` threaded through the lowerer and the target rules, DDB-229 for a rewrite a target cannot do without, `-- rewrites off:` in the lowered query and every load script, `rewrites` in the metadata documents and schemas, `dbdatabuild matrix --rewrites`.
- **Language areas** (docs/research/template-findings.md, L1 to L9): PIVOT tables found by DuckDB's parser, UNPIVOT lowered (a lateral VALUES list over one scan: CROSS APPLY on SQL Server, LATERAL on PostgreSQL), quantiles and median ranked (DECIMAL results cut as DuckDB does), `nth_value`, JSON extraction on both engines, regular expressions on PostgreSQL; eight new template models; probes for JSON and regular expressions.
- **Verified**: 1,414 unit tests; on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 the four templates equal DuckDB's rows (eight runs), `retail` and `adventureworks` built with `fidelity: native` have DuckDB's row counts (four runs), and `RewriteOptOutProbes` shows the engine's own answer differs from DuckDB's, in the documented way, for eleven optional rewrites. Fabric has never run.
- **Left**: `UNNEST` of list columns, lateral and date series, regular expressions and the remaining JSON functions on SQL Server.

## 48. Oracle, Spark SQL and the BigQuery emulator as probed engines; the conformance suite in groups

- **Built**: `IProbeEngine` (what a probe needs: a table of the eight seeded rows and a query) with `Engine` (SQL Server, PostgreSQL) and three new probe-only engines: Oracle 23ai Free (Oracle.ManagedDataAccess), Spark SQL 4.0
  (Thrift server in http mode, read with the ADBC Spark driver) and the BigQuery emulator (REST `jobs.query`). `Dialects.ForTarget` knows `oracle`, `spark` and `bigquery`. The probes run on all five; the three new ones
  against a baseline of the cases that agree with DuckDB (`Baselines/<engine>.txt`; the test fails on a regression, `DDB_PROBE_BASELINE=update` rewrites it). `scripts/test-engines.sh up [mssql pg oracle spark bigquery | all]`.
- **Scoreboard** (no rewrite rules yet, polyglot's own translation): Spark 225 of 267 agree, BigQuery emulator 163, Oracle 113; the differences are listed in `docs/research/engine-differences/oracle-spark-bigquery.md`.
- **Test groups** (the conformance suite took 6 min 17 s in one run): traits `Group=apply` (the plan and apply loop, about 2 min), `Group=templates` (the sample projects against DuckDB, about 2.5 min), `Group=release`
  (the sample projects with the rewrites off), the rest is the quick group (about 1.5 min). `ci.yml` runs the groups and each preview engine as parallel jobs; `conformance-full.yml` (weekly, on request, and before a release:
  `release.yml` needs it) runs everything with every engine.
- **Left**: rules, matrix columns and refusals for the three dialects, in the order of the scoreboard; then the targets themselves (DDL types, tracking tables, load strategies, a driver behind `MutationGate`).

## 49. First target rules for Spark SQL, BigQuery and Oracle

- **Built**: sixteen rules in `TargetRules` and `RewriteCatalog` (the catalog test requires every rule to be listed): Spark `division-by-zero` (Infinity, -Infinity, NaN as DuckDB), `concat-skips-null`, `substring-bounds` (position 0, negative length, negative `left`), `week-of-year`, `regexp-replace-flags`, and the existing `split-part`, `date-diff-boundaries`, `date-diff-weeks`, `weekday-datefirst`, `double-to-int`, `regexp-full-match` now also for Spark; BigQuery `mod-function`, `date-diff-argument-order`, `date-diff-weeks`; Oracle `mod-function`, `varchar-length`, `left-right-substr`. `TargetRules.Finish` replaces `ddb_` markers after the transpile (polyglot turns `MOD(a, b)` back into `%`, and prints VARCHAR as CLOB on Oracle); called by the planner, the renderer and the probes.
- **Scoreboard** (docs/research/engine-differences/oracle-spark-bigquery.md): Spark 225 to 253 of 267 agree, BigQuery emulator 163 to 175, Oracle 113 to 119; `Baselines/*.txt` raised. Oracle's gain is small because the empty string is NULL there, which changes about forty probes at once.
- **Verified**: 1,432 unit tests; the three probe suites against the real engines (Spark 4.0, the emulator, Oracle 23ai Free). Not touched: SQL Server and PostgreSQL rules (their probes unchanged).
- **Left**: the rest of the lists in the scoreboard document; a matrix column for each engine; Oracle's empty-string rule; the BigQuery probe run took 6 to 9 minutes in three runs where it took 33 seconds in another (emulator slowness on failing queries?): check before the CI job's 30 minute limit matters.

## 50. `dbdatabuild mcp`: the commands as tools for an AI agent

- **Built** (DESIGN.md 9.7): a Model Context Protocol server on standard input and output (`src/DbDataBuild.Cli/Mcp/`: `McpServer`, `Resources`, `McpCommand`; hand-written JSON-RPC, no package). Tools from the TUI's command catalog, each run in process with `--format json`; resources for the skill, the schemas and `explain`; progress notifications. `mcp` is the second command with no JSON form of its own (like `tui`); the tests that list commands know it.
- **Safety**: commands that change a target or its tracking tables are not offered without `--allow-writes`; `--project`, `diff --show-values` and `sample --data` are never offered; paths and model names outside the project are refused before anything runs; a test checks that no offered tool has an input that shows values or names the project.
- **Verified**: 1,441 unit tests (nine new in `McpServerTests`, including the stdin loop and a line that is not JSON); a real run of the built executable over a pipe (initialize, then `graph`). Not tried against Claude Desktop, VS Code or another host.
- **Left**: prompts (the skill's workflows), cancelling a running command (the loop is sequential: a request is read after the previous one finishes), an `.mcpb` bundle in the release job, a test that no tool *result* sent to a model carries row values (today by construction: the withheld options), outputSchema per tool, the UI extension.

## 51. `dbdatabuild web`: the read-only web interface

- **Built** (DESIGN.md 9.7): a loopback HTTP server (`HttpListener`, no new package) and one embedded page (`Web/index.html`, plain script, no toolchain): Health, Lineage (an SVG layered graph with upstream and downstream highlighting and column lineage), Models (columns, query, lowered, rendered per target), Tests, Support matrix. Two endpoints: `POST /api/run` for the read-only commands and `GET /api/file` for project files. `ToolSurface` (split out of `McpServer`) is the shared rule for which inputs a client may use. `web` is the third command with no JSON form.
- **Safety**: token in the address and in a header, Host and Origin checks, loopback only, a content security policy, no CORS, files only from the project's own directories and never through a link, write flags withheld, one command at a time. Eight tests in `WebServerTests` start a real server and try the refusals (no token, wrong host, wrong origin, wrong content type, commands that write, paths that leave the project, `.env`, plans, a symbolic link out).
- **Verified**: 1,448 unit tests; the page loaded in headless Chrome against the retail template (all five screens render, no script errors, screenshots read). Not tried on Windows or with a browser other than Chrome; the keyboard and screen-reader behaviour has not been looked at.
- **Left**: the lowered and rendered tabs need the files written by `render --write` (a command that returns the text without writing would remove that); plan review, apply and diff; a test that scripts the page (today only the server is tested and the page is checked by hand); the page as an MCP app; paging for a very large project's graph.

## 52. `render --content`, and prompts for the MCP server

- **Built**: `render --content` puts each rendered file's text in the JSON document (`data.files[].content`; plain render only, schema `filesHashedWithContent`), so a client can show the lowered query and the rendered scripts of the current models without `render --write`. The web page's Lowered and per-target tabs use it (they no longer read `rendered/`, which may be stale or unwritten), and so does any MCP client (`render` tool, input `content`). `prompts/list` and `prompts/get` on the MCP server: `explore-project`, `add-model`, `change-model`, `fix-findings`, `review-plan` (`Prompts.cs`); a test checks that every command a prompt names is a tool the server offers. The skill mentions `web` and `mcp`.
- **Verified**: 1,449 unit tests (render content with nothing written, the prompts); the page's two tabs read in headless Chrome. The prompts were not tried in a host (Claude Desktop shows prompts as slash commands; unverified).
- **Left**: cancelling a running MCP command, outputSchema per tool, the `.mcpb` bundle, plan review and apply in the page, a scripted test of the page.

## 53. `review`, the Plans screen, MCP cancellation, a scripted test of the page

- **Built**: `dbdatabuild review [plan]` (offline): lists the project's plans (newest first, an edited one marked not intact) or shows one with steps, risk, reasons, parameters, exact statements, the report and the allowances `apply` would need; it reads plans the way `apply` does and applies nothing (`PlanBrowser`, schema `data_review`). It is a web screen (Plans), an MCP tool and the thing the `review-plan` prompt uses (before it, an agent had no way to read a plan: the file tools of the server did not exist). MCP: `notifications/cancelled` for the running request reaches the command's stop hook and the reply is dropped, as the protocol says; a second thread reads input while a command runs (`McpServerTests` with a command that waits to be stopped). `WebPageTests`: the page is loaded in headless Chrome against a real server and each screen is read back from the DOM (health, lineage, a model's tabs, plans, tests, the matrix; text from the project that looks like markup stays text); they are reported as skipped where no Chrome is on the path.
- **Verified**: 1,457 unit tests (the page tests ran here with Chrome; on CI they run on ubuntu, which has Chrome, and are skipped on Windows); the Plans screen read in a screenshot.
- **Not built, on purpose**: apply from the page or an MCP tool. It changes a database, so it needs the design of the approval (the plan hash, the named allowances, who may press the button, what the model can and cannot see) agreed first.
- **Also**: a first accessibility pass over the page (clickable elements that are not links or buttons take Tab, Enter and Space, with a visible focus ring; the graph nodes too; labels on the filters; the current screen and tab marked with `aria-current`; the main area is a polite live region). Done from the markup and the tests, not with a screen reader.
- **Left**: outputSchema per tool; an answers form for `plan` questions; `.mcpb` packaging (the manifest format has to be checked against the current specification first); a pass with a real screen reader; the page as an MCP app.

## 54. Answers and apply from the page, and approval for an agent's writes

- **Built**: in `dbdatabuild web`: *Make a plan* (`plan` from the page), the questions form (each question with options, consequences, the proposal; answers written to `.dbdatabuild/web-answers.yml`; the plan is made again), and, with `--allow-apply`, *Apply* (named allowances, the target typed, a dry run, a background job with progress and a stop request; `WebActions`: `/api/answers`, `/api/apply`, `/api/job`, `/api/job/stop`, `/api/capabilities`). The server re-reads the plan and checks the confirmation and the allowances itself. In the MCP server, a run of a command that changes a database is put to the person through the host (elicitation) and needs their approval plus the command's name typed back; without elicitation support it is refused with the command to run by hand. `ToolSurface` gained per-surface withheld options; the MCP `apply` tool's plan path is made absolute from the project (it used to be resolved from the server's working directory).
- **Verified**: 1,473 unit tests (the refusals: no `--allow-apply`, wrong target or plan id, an allowance the plan does not need, an edited plan, a second apply while one runs, stop; the answers file; the elicitation: approve, decline, cancel, not approved, wrong text, a host that cannot ask, a dry run); the page driven in headless Chrome through the questions and the apply panel (a scripted session with a stand-in host: the Apply button stays disabled until the target is typed and each allowance ticked; the answers file was written; the job's progress and result were shown); **on real engines**: `The_web_page_applies_a_plan_only_when_the_person_confirmed_it` on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 (unconfirmed refused with the database untouched, a dry run changes nothing, a confirmed apply creates and loads the tables, a second apply is refused by the command); the apply group (48 tests) passes.
- **Not verified**: elicitation in a real host (Claude Desktop, VS Code): the shape of the request follows the 2025-06-18 specification, which was not checked against a host. The questions form was driven with a stand-in `plan`, not against a model change that really asks.
- **Left**: `apply --resume` and `--allow-dirty` from the page; table diff and sample data in the page; the page as an MCP app (where the approval would live in the frame and the tool could be hidden from the model); the `.mcpb` bundle.

## 55. Sample data and table diff in the page

- **Built**: two screens in `dbdatabuild web`: Sample data (`sample`: models, rows, seed, limit, scale, the sources too; tables with their rows) and Table diff (`diff`: counts, key, columns, type differences, differing rows by column, column statistics, sample rows only with "Show values"). `ToolSurface(personReads: true)` offers `diff --show-values` and `sample --data` to the page and still withholds them from the MCP server.
- **Verified**: 1,474 unit tests (sample runs, diff runs, values asked for by a person); `WebApplyConformanceTests` also runs the page's diff on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 (identical, then one amount changed: one differing row, no samples without `show_values`, the new value with it); the Sample screen read in a screenshot after a real run, the Table diff screen after feeding it a document of the schema's shape.
- **Left**: diff from the page for views of another target (a diff is within one target); `sample --data` from the page needs a CSV directory in the project and was not tried.
- **Also fixed**: `WebServer` chose a free port and bound it later, so two servers started at once (the test suite does) could meet on one port ("Address already in use"); it now chooses again when the bind fails. Found as a flaky test after the first push of this entry; the repeated unit runs after the fix pass.

## 56. The page as an MCP app

- **Built**: the page is also an MCP app. `WebBackend` (split out of `WebServer`) holds everything a person's interface asks of a project; the HTTP server and the MCP server are two carriers of it. In the page, `call(op, payload)` is the only place that knows the carrier (`__TRANSPORT__`: `http` or `mcp`); the MCP carrier does the app's side of the specification's postMessage protocol (`ui/initialize`, `ui/notifications/initialized`, `tools/call` through the host, `ui/notifications/tool-result` to open the right screen, host theme, `size-changed`, `ping`, `ui/resource-teardown`). The server: the `ui://dbdatabuild/app` resource, `_meta.ui.resourceUri` (and the older `ui/resourceUri` key) on `review`, `plan`, `graph`, `diff`, `sample`, a `show` tool, and the app-only `ui_*` tools; none of it is offered to a host that did not advertise the extension. `dbdatabuild mcp --allow-apply`: the person's apply button in the app (typed target, named allowances), independent of `--allow-writes`. `scripts/mcp-app-host.mjs`: a stand-in host.
- **Verified**: 1,479 unit tests (nothing offered without the extension and `ui_` tools unknown; visibility `["app"]` on every `ui_` tool; the resource's type and content; `ui_run`, `ui_file` refusals like the page's; `ui_apply` refused without `--allow-apply`, without the typed target, run when confirmed, and no `apply` tool for the model); in a browser with the stand-in host: the app loads in a sandboxed iframe, completes `ui/initialize`, a model's `show(plans)` opens the Plans screen, the host's theme switch re-colours it, its calls go to the server as `ui_run`/`ui_capabilities`, and in the app a dry run goes through `ui_apply` and `ui_job` to its result (the real command refused it: no database login in that environment).
- **Not verified**: a real host. The message flow follows the extension's specification as published (2026-01-26) and was read from it, not tested against Claude Desktop or VS Code; hosts differ in what they implement (display modes, the legacy resource key, whether a result of an app's tool call also reaches the model). The stand-in host proves the app and the server agree, not that a given host agrees with the specification.
- **Left**: try it in Claude Desktop and VS Code; a CSP entry if a host needs one; the `.mcpb` bundle (a place to start the server from: `dbdatabuild mcp --project ... --allow-apply`).

## 57. The app against the reference host; connecting a host; the `.mcpb` bundle

- **Tried**: the reference host of the extension (`basic-host` of modelcontextprotocol/ext-apps: the SDK's AppBridge, a double sandbox, an independent implementation of the specification) against `dbdatabuild mcp`, through a stdio-to-HTTP bridge (`supergateway`). **Finding**: it connects with no capabilities, so it does not advertise `io.modelcontextprotocol/ui`, and the server (which shows the app and its app-only tools only to a client that does, as the specification says a server SHOULD, and because a host that does not know the extension would show those tools to the model) offered neither. A small adapter (`scripts/mcp-advertise-ui.mjs`) adds the capability to the `initialize` request for such hosts; it is for trying the app, not a setting of the product. With it: the host listed `show`, `review`, `plan`, `graph`, `diff`, `sample` and not one `ui_` tool (it hides app-only tools), a call of `show` with `{"screen":"plans"}` rendered the Plans screen in the host's sandbox, a call of `review` with a plan opened that plan with its apply panel (so `ui_capabilities`, `ui_run` and `ui_file` went through the SDK's bridge). The stand-in host and the reference host agree.
- **Written**: `docs/interfaces.md` (the four interfaces, `--allow-writes` against `--allow-apply`, the settings for Claude Desktop and VS Code, how to try the app with the stand-in and the reference host, and a checklist of what differs between hosts to look at in a real one), a README section, `packaging/mcpb/manifest.json` and `scripts/pack-mcpb.sh` (an `.mcpb` bundle from a published executable: the manifest passes the vendor's validator, `npx @anthropic-ai/mcpb validate`; the bundle is read-only and asks only for the project folder). The server reports the build's version (the one `publish.sh` stamps) instead of the product's constant.
- **Not verified**: Claude Desktop and VS Code (nobody could open them from the build machine), installing the `.mcpb` bundle in a host, a Windows bundle. The bundle is not attached to the release until a host has installed it.
- **Left**: a real-host pass with the checklist; attach the bundle to releases after it; `ProductInfo.Version` is still "0.1.0" (it is in every rendered header, so changing it re-renders every project's files: its own decision).

## 58. SQL Server 2025 in the suite, what `version` means, and the regular expressions

- **Built**: a SQL Server 2025 engine in the suite (`scripts/test-engines.sh up mssql2025`, `DBDATABUILD_TEST_MSSQL2025`, the CI group `mssql2025`, and `up all` in the full run); the dialect probes run on it twice, as `sqlserver2025` (database compatibility level 170, told to the tool as version 17) and `sqlserver2025-160` (level 160, version 16); `TargetRules.Apply` takes the configured version, and from 17 SQL Server gets the rules `regexp-full-match` (anchored), `regexp-extract` (`REGEXP_SUBSTR` with the group, `''` for no match or a group that did not take part) and `regexp-replace-first` (`REGEXP_REPLACE` with occurrence 1, or 0 for `'g'`); the matrix rows `fn.regexp_full_match`, `fn.regexp_extract`, `fn.regexp_replace` are `translated, min_version: 17` (the matrix hash changed: goldens regenerated), `fn.regexp` says REGEXP_LIKE needs level 170. Four probes added (first and every match over several matches, a group that did not take part, an alternation in a full match); the Planner, renderer, checks and probes all pass the version to the rules.
- **Findings on a 2025 server**: at level 160 `REGEXP_LIKE` and `REGEXP_SUBSTR` are not recognized but `REGEXP_REPLACE` works, so 2025@160 can do more than 2022 and never less: the single `version` setting (the T-SQL level to generate for; 16 for 2022 and for 2025@160, 17 for 2025@170) is enough, as concluded earlier; the default replace replaces every match (DuckDB: the first); REGEXP_SUBSTR gives NULL where DuckDB gives `''`; `REGEXP_LIKE` is case-sensitive by default whatever the database collation; the transpile rewrites a call of `regexp_replace` as DuckDB's four-argument form and drops what it does not know, so the rule writes a marker name (`ddb_regexp_replace`, spelled out by `TargetRules.Finish`). The whole conformance suite (quick, apply, templates; 109 tests) also passes with the 2025 container standing in for the 2022 one.
- **Verified**: 1,486 unit tests; probes on 2022, 2025@170 and 2025@160 (every regular expression probe agrees with DuckDB at 170, and 2025@160 behaves as 2022 for a project at 16); `A_project_at_version_17_loads_regular_expressions_on_SQL_Server_2025_and_gets_DuckDBs_rows` (init, render, check, plan and apply on 2025@170 and the table equals DuckDB's rows).
- **Not done**: the other things 2025 adds (a native JSON type, vectors) are not used, and the probes found no other difference (the probe corpus is what we have); the `i` and `s` regular expression options; Fabric.

## 59. Oracle's empty string: measured, classified, designed

- **Built**: the probes know an engine that has no empty string (`IProbeEngine.EmptyStringIsNull`, true for Oracle): a case whose answers differ only on the row that holds `''` (id 4) is reported as `empty-string`, a class apart from `DIFFERENT`; five probes about it (`s IS NULL`, `s = ''`, `coalesce`, a `CASE`).
- **Measured** (Oracle 23ai Free): 90 probe cases differ from DuckDB; **36 of them differ only on the empty-string row**, 54 are something else (the summary was `DIFFERENT 91` before, with the cause hidden in all of them), 59 are errors. `Baselines/oracle.txt` unchanged in size (119 agree).
- **Designed, not built** (DESIGN.md 7.4, "Empty strings"): Oracle cannot be made to keep `''`, so when it becomes a target it gets a matrix row `str.empty_string` that is `approximated`, an error unless the project accepts it (`string_semantics.empty_string: null`); a NOT NULL text column, and a unique key or grain over a text column, are refused for an Oracle target; a model test for it may say `empty_string: null`. Nothing here is implemented, because Oracle is not in the matrix (three target columns) and not a target.
- **Left for Oracle**: the 54 other differences and the 59 errors (booleans in a select list, date parts, `split_part`, `regexp_extract`, `starts_with`, `concat_ws`, `json_*`, `greatest` with NULL); then the target itself.

## 60. The skill for an agent with the tools, and `agent-kit --mcp`

- **Built**: a section in the skill, "If the dbdatabuild tools are available": the host starts the server and the agent cannot; use the tools instead of the shell; what is not offered and why (database-changing commands, `--show-values`, `sample --data`, paths outside the project) and not to route around a refusal; open the interface (`show`, `review`, `plan`, `graph`, `diff`, `sample`) when the person should look or decide; the Apply button and the host's confirmation are the person's; the prompts. `dbdatabuild agent-kit --write --mcp` (opt-in) adds the dbdatabuild server to the project's `.mcp.json`: `type: stdio`, `command: dbdatabuild`, `args: [mcp, --project, .]`, and the READ logins passed on by name (`${DBDATABUILD_<TARGET>_READ:-}`); other servers and keys in the file are kept, the write is atomic and LF, a second run changes nothing, `--check` checks it, and a file that is not a JSON object (or whose `mcpServers` is not one) is refused and left alone. Without `--mcp` the file is never touched. The JSON document has a `mcp` part (schema updated).
- **Checked against Claude Code's documentation** (not run in Claude Code): the file is `.mcp.json` at the project root with a top-level `mcpServers`; a stdio entry has `type`, `command`, `args`, `env`; `${VAR}` and `${VAR:-default}` expand in `command`, `args` and `env` values; stdio servers start in the project directory; project servers need the user's approval in an interactive session.
- **Verified**: 1,493 unit tests (opt-in, read-only content, no write login in the file, other servers kept, idempotent, `--check`, three kinds of file refused and untouched, the skill's section and the screens it names, the schema).
- **Not verified**: that Claude Code accepts `${VAR:-}` with an empty default for an unset login (the documentation shows defaults with a value; the server treats an empty login as missing), and the whole flow in a real Claude Code session.

## 61. Nothing that changes with a release in a rendered file

- **Changed**: the rendered load scripts and resolvers no longer have `-- matrix version:` and `-- tool version:` lines, `manifest.yml` no longer has `matrix_version:` and `tool_version:`, and `lowered.sql` no longer has `-- tool version:` (it keeps `-- duckdb:`, the one input of the lowering that is not in the project: an upgrade shows up as a diff on purpose). Reason: a rendered file is a function of the model, the project configuration and the translation rules; whatever in the rule code changes the output changes the text, and the `target rules:` and `type rules:` lines name what fired. A version in the header added no information and made every file of every project differ, with the same SQL, after each release (and after any edit of any matrix row, for the matrix hash), which failed `render --check` in every project's CI and forced a re-render. Nothing read those lines back: they were only written (checked in the code before removing them). The matrix status that applies to a model is in the manifest, per operation.
- **`ProductInfo.Version` follows the release** now: it is the assembly's version, which `scripts/publish.sh` stamps (`VERSION`), and a development build has the one in `Directory.Build.props` (0.3.0), which a release bumps. It is in plans, the JSON documents and the tracking tables as provenance. (It had stayed `0.1.0` since v0.1.0, which is why v0.2.0 and v0.3.0 said 0.1.0 in their documents.)
- **Effect on users**: a project that upgrades re-renders once (`render --write`); the SQL in the scripts is unchanged, the script hashes in the manifests change because the headers are shorter. After that an upgrade changes a rendered file only if its text changes.
- **Verified**: 1,493 unit tests; the goldens differ only by the removed lines and the hashes that follow from them.
- **Left**: a release now bumps `Directory.Build.props`.

## 62. Running queries on one server and writing the results to another: investigation

- **Done** (`docs/research/cross-server.md`; no product code changed): what the code assumes (a target is an engine kind with one read and one write login; sources have no location; a three-part name is refused by the lowering, DDB-324; the gate takes text statements and its one bulk form is a parameterised `INSERT`), and experiments on real engines. **Measured**: SQL Server cross-database names work in a transaction; PostgreSQL has no cross-database queries; a SQL Server linked server pulls fine inside a transaction (1M rows in 8.5 s) but a push inside one fails for lack of a distributed transaction (Linux; Windows would need MSDTC); `postgres_fdw` pulls and pushes inside transactions; DuckDB with its PostgreSQL and community `mssql` extensions moves PostgreSQL to SQL Server (1M rows, 5.1 s) and SQL Server to SQL Server (7.2 s) with DuckDB's own semantics; client-side copy is 190k rows/s with `SqlBulkCopy` against 10k rows/s with the gate's parameterised inserts, so a transfer needs a real bulk API; drivers return different types for one column (`DateOnly` against `DateTime`; 28-digit `decimal`).
- **Proposed**: named connections (`connections:`, source-only connections), a source location with `link` access (physical names at render time, the link checked not created), and a `transfer` step (a query run on one connection, rows bulk-written to a staging table on another, then the existing load strategies) with a `BulkCopy` gate statement; compute at the source later; DuckDB in the middle rejected as a load path (bypasses the gate). Order A (same-server other database), B (links), C (connections), D (transfer), E (compute at the source). Five decisions for the owner are at the end of the note.
- **Left**: everything above is a proposal; the owner's answers on which scenarios come first, whether rows may pass through the machine running the tool, and Fabric decide the order.

## 63. Direction for cross-server work, and a draft of domains

- **Decided by the owner**: no linked servers or foreign servers, no DuckDB in the middle, no support for same-server cross-database queries (views and synonyms outside the tool), connections generalised, and the tool moving the data between connections itself.
- **Drafted** (`docs/research/cross-server.md`, "Domains"): several domains (self-contained folders with their own models, sources and targets) in one repository, with connections defined once at the root, explicit pairing by declaration (`land` on a source, `publish` on a model, never a product of all sources and targets), published outputs as persisted contracts whose consumer-side descriptors are generated and validated offline across domains, and fan-out of raw and transformed data. Open points in the note: names (a domain as a DuckDB catalog), tracking tables per domain, plans and run order across edges, least-privilege connections per domain, incremental movement, partial fan-out and its reconciliation.
- **Left**: no code; the note ends in the decisions that fix the first step.

## 64. Terms and design for connections, copies and fan-in (agreed on paper)

- **Settled with the owner** (`docs/research/cross-server.md`, "Terms" down): a clean break; the project stays the only unit; **connection** (a named database endpoint) replaces "target" for where a model lives and **engine** is the dialect; every table is a **model**, with kinds that build from SQL, **`mapped`** (no body: maps an existing physical table; replaces "source") and **`copy`** (no SQL: `from:` a model on another connection, columns inherited); a query runs against one connection and moving data is always an explicit copy; configuration inherits root, then `_dbdatabuild.yml` per folder, then the model (nearest wins, explicit merge rules, provenance shown); fan-in is a model that exists on several connections (a model's `connections` list, usually set once by a folder's `_dbdatabuild.yml`; there is no separate "group") and a copy that reads from each, with one word, **parameter**, at every scope (project, connection, origin, model, operation; a prefix when referenced: `${origin.store_id}`) used as an added column or as the slice an origin replaces, never a dictated column; per-origin isolation and version-skew reporting. A copy has at most one dimension of expansion. Every folder's `_dbdatabuild.yml` is a project file with the same sections as the root one, layered root-down, with explicit merge rules (`=`, `-`, `+` suffixes) and shown provenance; model parameters are only in the model's file.
- **No code yet.** The note lists the order (connections and the rename; copy with a bulk gate statement; groups and slices; incremental extraction) and four open points (column or row selection in a copy, attribute syntax, drift checks of mapped models at plan, command and key names).
- **Left**: the first build step is the connections-and-rename change, which touches every place that treats a target as an engine; it is a major version.

## 65. Tracking decided: nothing by default, centrally if configured

- **Decided with the owner** (`docs/research/cross-server.md`, "Tracking"): tracking is a setting, not a property of the connection it describes. Default: nothing is tracked, with a **warning** on any command that would have recorded something and no tracking configured; a configured tracking connection that is unreachable is an **error** before anything is touched; offline tracking and catch-up come later. Where: `tracking.connection` and `schema` at the root, overridable per connection (`tracking: none` is the explicit opt-out), never per folder; every tracking row carries its `connection` in its key, so one tracking connection can hold everything; `init` creates the tables once per tracking connection; a plan's steps name their connection and `apply` checks every connection it will use before the first statement. The restore-and-clone worry that argued for local tracking is real only for the data-coupled tables (`run_log` hashes, `operation_interval`) and can be met by cheap checks against the live data; drift fails safe against a central baseline.
- **Written down, not built**: what the tool cannot do with no tracking (drift, the incremental definition block, acknowledgements, history, `report`, `publish-metadata`), and three points the owner then accepted: with no tracking, plans are made from declared against live and changes to existing objects are marked risky; `tracking: none` is the opt-out; no copies to further tracking connections in the first version.
- **Left**: no code.


## 66. Connections in the configuration (first build step of the cross-server work)

- **Built**: the project file's `connections:` section replaces `targets:`. A connection is a name with an `engine` (`sqlserver`, `postgres`, `fabric`) and an optional `version`; its logins are `DBDATABUILD_<NAME>_READ` and `_WRITE`, with no fallback from one connection or login to another. A connection named after an engine exists without a declaration (declaring it only sets the version), which keeps existing projects close to unchanged. Names are letters, digits and underscores, unique ignoring case. Everything that chooses behaviour (dialect, matrix row, rewrite rules, types, tracking DDL, catalog reads) now asks for the connection's engine; rendered files, plans and logins use the connection's name; two connections of one engine can have different versions and so different rewrite rules.
- **Still named as before** (to be renamed in the next step): `targets:` on models, operations, indexes and hooks, `default_targets`, `--target`, `target` in the JSON documents, `TargetNames`. These now hold connection names.
- **Deviation from the agreed clean break**: engine-named implicit connections remain, to limit churn until the rename step. Hook `script:` maps are still keyed by engine.
- **Verified**: unit suite (1508, including `ConnectionsTests`: parsing and refusals, engine lookup, login variable names, rendering two same-engine connections and a PostgreSQL one into separate directories, version-specific rules per connection). Not yet run against two real servers.
- **Left**: the rename; layered `_dbdatabuild.yml` files; `mapped` models; tracking section; `copy`; fan-in; parameters.

## 67. The vocabulary follows the connections (rename)

- **Renamed** (a clean break, no aliases): `--target` is `--connection` on every command; `targets:` on models, operations, indexes and hooks is `connections:`; `default_targets` is `default_connections`; the `target` field of the JSON documents, the plan file header, the rendered header (`-- connection:`) and `manifest.yml` is `connection`; model selector `target:` is `connection:`; the metadata documents and the `metadata_*` views say `connections` and `connection` (`metadata_loads`, `metadata_hooks`, `metadata_native_types`); the define question `Q-define-<model>-targets` is `Q-define-<model>-connections` (questions are asked in id order, so it now comes before `kind`). Rewrites and the construct matrix are per **engine**, so their JSON says `engines` (they said `targets`). `plan` objects, the `review` list, the web page and the TUI chooser use connection names; the `--connection` option takes any project connection name (the TUI form takes it as text), and `agent-kit --mcp` passes on a read login for each connection of the project.
- **Not renamed**: the C# names (`Target`, `TargetNames`, `TargetRules`, `ProjectContext.TargetsOf`), the effect classes ("target reads"), and the engine-named implicit connections.
- **Verified**: unit suite (1508) and the conformance groups other than `release` (111 passed, 2 skipped) on SQL Server 2022/2025 and PostgreSQL 17. Not run: the `release` group, Fabric.

## 68. Project files in folders, and the merge rules

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 9.4.1): `models/**/_dbdatabuild.yml` with a `defaults:` section layered between the root `dbdatabuild.yml` `defaults:` and a model's own file, the nearest winning. `default_connections` is gone: it is `defaults: {connections: [...]}` (a clean break). The merge is `YamlMerge` with one table of rules (maps merge by key, lists append with a scalar kept once, a list of mappings with a `name` merges by name, scalars replace; suffixes `=` replace, `-` remove, `+` merge; a suffix on a single value or a list-over-mapping is an error) and a unit test per rule. Layered model keys: `connections`, `kind`, `hooks`, `rewrites`, `lint_ignore`. `kind` may now come from a folder, so the model schema requires only `name` and `columns`.
- **Lists append, so a model that wants only its own connections writes `connections=:`**; `define` writes that for the connections it asks about, so a generated file means the same under any defaults.
- **Provenance**: a diagnostic about an inherited setting names the file and line it is in; `validate` prints what each model inherited and from where; the model metadata document has `inherited`. A folder file is fuzzed with the other inputs.
- **Verified**: unit suite (1546). Real engines not needed (no SQL changes); the conformance groups were last run at entry 67.
- **Left**: parameters, tags, `schema` as a setting, project-wide sections in a folder file (a folder file may only hold `defaults:`), `define` ignores inheritance when it reads an existing definition, and the effective settings are shown per model only where something was inherited.

## 69. Mapped models replace sources

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5): a table that exists and that the tool does not build is a **mapped model**: `models/<schema>/<table>.yml` with `kind: {type: mapped}` and no `.sql`. `sources/` is no longer read; if it exists, `validate` says where its files go (`models/<schema>/<table>.yml`, plus the kind). The file may get `kind` and `connections` from a folder's `_dbdatabuild.yml`, so a folder named `sources` (or anything) with `defaults: {kind: {type: mapped}}` is the old layout in one line. A `.sql` beside a mapped model is an error; `define` skips mapped models (and says so when one is named). `import-sources` writes mapped models (new ones with `connections=: [<connection>]`; a refresh keeps what the file said). The templates were moved (`sources/<s>` to `models/<s>`, kind added).
- **Editors**: `model.schema.json` hands a file that says `kind: {type: mapped}` to `source.schema.json` (if/then/else), so one glob serves both; `kind` is no longer required by the model schema (it may be inherited) but still by the loader.
- **Not renamed yet**: the command `import-sources` (to `import`), the JSON key `sources`, the metadata document kind `dbdatabuild.source/1` and the `metadata_source*` views; the C# names.
- **Not built**: a check that a model's connections have every mapped table it reads (`connections` of a mapped model is recorded, not enforced); that belongs with `copy`.
- **Verified**: unit suite (1555) and all conformance groups on SQL Server 2022/2025 and PostgreSQL 17 (111 + 4 release, 2 skipped).

## 70. `import-sources` is `import`

- **Renamed** (no alias): the command that exports tables from a connection as mapped models is `dbdatabuild import`. Everything that named it follows (specs, schema, TUI, MCP tool, skill, docs). The JSON key `sources` of its document and the C# names stay for now.
- **Verified**: unit suite and the import conformance test on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17.

## 71. Copies: rows between connections

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.2): `kind: {type: copy, from: schema.table}`. No `.sql`, no `columns` (the origin's), the origin on one connection and the copy on others. A copy is a `full_replace` load over a staging table in the tracking schema, whose generated query and generated mapped table make it flow through lowering, rendering and planning unchanged. `plan` adds a `transfer` step (staging DDL, origin connection, exact read, declared columns), then the load, then a drop of the staging table; `apply` checks and opens every origin before its first statement, streams the one SELECT through `ReadSession`, converts each value by declared logical type (`TransferValues`; errors name the column, not the value) and writes with `GateStatement.BulkCopy` (`SqlBulkCopy` in batches of 20,000, Npgsql binary `COPY`) through the gate, logged as `transfer` with a row count and no values. The staging DDL and the bulk statement are in the dry run. `GateInvariantTests` now also scans for the bulk APIs.
- **Driver setting**: Npgsql's conversion of 0001-01-01 and 9999-12-31 to `-infinity`/`infinity` is off for every connection (`DriverSettings`, called first by the command line and by `LoginSettings`), because a warehouse's "no end date" is a date. A truly infinite value is now an error on read.
- **Plan file**: steps may carry a `transfer:` block (schema and parser updated).
- **Verified on real engines** (`CopyConformanceTests`): SQL Server to PostgreSQL and back, 11 columns of every logical type family, NULLs in every column, unicode, an empty string, a 28-digit decimal, dates and timestamps at their limits, a quote and a newline in text; a dry run creates nothing; the staging table is gone after; a second plan has no DDL; a re-run replaces; 300,000 rows in either direction (about 4 to 6 seconds end to end: plan applied, transfer, load, index); a missing origin login is refused with nothing executed; a decimal wider than the declared type stops the transfer and leaves the destination table empty. Unit tests: planning, conversion, gate logging.
- **Not verified**: Fabric; large objects; time zones other than UTC for `TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE`.
- **Left**: fan-in (several origins), incremental copies, an origin shape check at `plan`, the check that a model's connections have the mapped tables it reads.

## 72. Fan-in: one application on several connections

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.2): connection `parameters:` (names to single values); `kind.slice` and `kind.on_mismatch` on a copy; a mapped model on several connections; the copy's columns gain the slice column when the origin lacks it (type required), the grain gains it, and the implicit load is `delete_insert_by_key` on it, so a run replaces only the loading origin's rows. References are `${origin.name}` (the origin's parameter) and `${connection.name}` (the destination's); other scopes are refused at load, and a missing parameter is named. The plan has a transfer and a load per origin (the transfer step carries `slice: {column, value, added}`; apply writes the value into every row or checks that every row holds it) and one staging drop. `plan` checks every mapped origin's live table against its declaration (DDB-230, new): a column gone, a type changed, a column that can now be NULL; `on_mismatch: fail` stops the plan, `skip` leaves that origin out with a warning; an origin with no login in the environment is a note. `apply` still refuses to start unless every origin opens.
- **Verified on real engines** (`CopyConformanceTests.One_application_on_several_systems_...`): two PostgreSQL databases as stores into SQL Server, overlapping order numbers kept apart by the slice, a row of no origin untouched by two runs, a store that lost a column named and refused, then skipped while the other origin loaded and the skipped one's rows stayed. Unit tests: validation (added or existing slice column, the refusals), planner steps per origin, plan-file round trip.
- **Left**: project parameters and folder-level overrides of connection parameters (the root file only for now), incremental copies, the origin check for an origin that is a built model, parameters in model SQL, `report` showing each origin's last good run.

## 73. A query runs on one connection: checked

- **Built**: `validate` and `plan` refuse a model that reads a table not on its connection (DDB-231, new): a mapped model declared on other connections, or a model or copy built elsewhere. The fix names the copy to add. A copy's generated staging table is on the copy's own connections. Unit tests: refused until copied, and the fine cases (a model on the table's own connection, a model reading a copy on another default connection).
- **Verified**: unit suite (1599) and the conformance groups except release (117, 2 skipped) on SQL Server 2022/2025 and PostgreSQL 17.

## 74. Tracking: central, per connection, and off by default

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 12): `tracking: { connection, schema }` at the project and `connections.<name>.tracking` (`none`, or another connection and schema); `tracking_schema` is gone. The records of a connection live on its tracking connection, which may be another one, with that connection's logins; **every tracking table has a `connection` column** (part of the key where there is one; layout version 4, an older layout is refused by name and cannot be upgraded in place) and every read filters by it. `ApplyEngine` holds a session and a gate per connection through a `Tracker` (a no-op when nothing is tracked); `plan` reads shapes from the data connection and records from the tracking one; `init` creates the tables on a connection that keeps records and tells any other where its records go; `ack`, `report`, `publish-metadata` work through the tracking connection and refuse an untracked one. The metadata views carry `connection`. The copy's staging DDL creates its schema.
- **Default is no tracking, with a warning** (DDB-232) on `plan` and `apply`; `tracking: none` is the silent choice. Untracked planning judges an existing object against the declaration alone and marks every DDL change to it risky (an added index excepted); no Track steps, no drift, no incremental-definition block, no resume (refused), no `ack`/`report`/`publish-metadata`. The templates now say `tracking: { connection: sqlserver }`.
- **Verified on real engines** (`CentralTrackingConformanceTests`): data on SQL Server with records on PostgreSQL (init once on the tracking connection, the data connection holds no record or schema, rows say `sqlserver`, a second plan finds the baseline on the other connection, an outside change is DDB-430, `ack` and `report` find the records), tracking off (nothing recorded anywhere, a new column is risky and needs `--allow-risky`, `report` refuses), and no section at all (the warning). The existing apply, template, copy and fan-in suites pass with `tracking:` added. Unit tests: config cases, init, tracking DDL.
- **Not built**: `copy_to` (records replicated to further connections), offline tracking and catching up, a layout upgrade from version 3.

## 75. Incremental copies

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.2): `unique_key` and `watermark: {column, lookback}` under a copy's `kind`. The origin read gains `WHERE <column> >= @watermark`; the bound is the destination's newest value of the column (per origin when there is a slice) less the lookback, worked out at plan time (a read of the destination in the plan's session) and recorded in the transfer step (`watermark: {column, type, value}`, schema and parser updated; bound by the driver, never in the text). Nothing in the destination reads everything. The load is `delete_insert_by_key` on the unique key (plus the slice column). `ReadSession.OpenStreamAsync` takes parameters. The model schema now describes a copy's `slice`, `on_mismatch`, `unique_key` and `watermark`.
- **Verified**: unit tests (validation, the refusals, the planner's read with and without a bound, the plan-file round trip); real engines, both directions (`An_incremental_copy_reads_only_what_changed...`): everything on the first run, then only the rows at or after the newest less the lookback, an edited row within the lookback replaced, a new row added, an old row whose column did not move not read again.
- **Left**: deletes at the origin are not seen (documented; a plain copy reconciles), no way to force a full refresh of one incremental copy short of removing its `watermark`.

## 76. Parameters

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.3): `parameters:` at the project (root file, overridable by each folder's `_dbdatabuild.yml`), per connection (`connections.<name>.parameters`, overridable by folder files too, which still cannot declare a connection) and per model (its own file only; refused in `defaults:`); a value is text or `{type, value}` (VARCHAR, BIGINT, INTEGER, SMALLINT, DATE, TIMESTAMP). References `${project.x}`, `${connection.x}`, `${model.x}` in a table model's query are **values**: markers while DuckDB binds and lowers (the text, its hash and the rendered files never depend on a value), placeholders `@p_<scope>_<name>` in the rendered script (declared with source `parameter`), filled at plan with the value on that connection and bound with its type at apply; never in statement text. The lowered artifact shows the references. A slice's value may use `project` and `model` references as well as `origin` and `connection`. `sample` and `test` run with the real values. Refusals: undefined, in a view, `origin` in a query, mixed types across connections, a clash with a marker, a marker DuckDB folds away. Schemas updated (config, model, plan `source: parameter`).
- **Verified**: unit tests (config forms and refusals, layering and overrides, scope isolation, rendered text on both engines, value change leaves files alone, the refusals, sample); real engines (`ParametersConformanceTests`, SQL Server and PostgreSQL): string, DATE, TIMESTAMP, BIGINT, INTEGER, SMALLINT and a model parameter bound in one query, only the row meeting every bound loaded, a new value gave new rows with `render --check` still clean, and the values are in `run_log`.
- **Not built (as agreed)**: parameters that change object names; boolean, decimal and double parameters; a view cannot use parameters.

## 77. Native models, and local copies

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.4; design `docs/research/native-queries.md`): `kind: {type: native, access: select, query: ...}` or a `.native.sql` beside the definition (never both): a table the engine computes from its own query, declared columns, exactly one connection, a single SELECT (the read guard moved to `Core`; T-SQL may not start with WITH). Queries bind it from the declared columns; on its connection it is **inlined after transpiling** as a derived table in a table's load script and in a view's DDL, its parameters (`${project.x}`, `${connection.x}`, its own `${model.x}`) as placeholders of its own (`@p_native_<model>__<scope>_<name>`) filled by the plan; a view cannot read one with parameters; another connection cannot read it (DDB-231). As a copy origin the native text is the origin read, its parameters carried in the transfer step (`transfer.parameters`, schema and parser updated). **Local copies**: a copy on the origin's own connection is an ordinary full-replace load over `SELECT cols FROM origin` (no staging, no transfer), for any model; mixed local and remote is refused, and `slice`/`unique_key`/`watermark` are refused on a local one. The source schema covers native models.
- **Verified**: unit tests (inline and file text, the refusals, derived-table splice with the alias, one-connection rule, local and remote copy classification, a view); real engines both ways (`NativeConformanceTests`): `STRING_SPLIT` and `unnest(string_to_array(...))` inline in a table and a view, bound parameter never in the script, a local copy of the native select, a changed value is a new plan with unchanged rendered files, and a PostgreSQL native select copied to SQL Server with its parameter bound on the origin.
- **Not built** (command: entry 78; change feeds are in the backlog, `OPEN-ITEMS.md` L): change feeds with deletes, `reads:` in the dependency graph (accepted, informational), the plan-time result-shape describe, `track_definition`, native models in the metadata documents.

## 78. Native commands

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.4): `kind: {type: native, access: command}` for a procedure or function call that returns rows. Needs `connections.<name>.allow_native_commands: true`; one call only (EXEC, EXECUTE, CALL or SELECT, no `;`); never readable by a query (DDB-231); only a copy origin, always a transfer, so a copy on the command's own connection works (the "local" form) through the staging table. Runs on the origin's read login in a transaction that is rolled back; result columns matched by name; the step is risky and `apply` refuses it when the connection does not allow commands. `@watermark` required in the text of an incremental copy.
- **Verified**: unit tests (`NativeCommandTests`: loading, refusals, no reading, transfer classification, watermark, plan step and round trip); real engines (`NativeConformanceTests`): SQL Server procedure and PostgreSQL function with a bound parameter, copied on the command's own connection, validate refusal, plan and dry run execute nothing, `--allow-risky` needed, a procedure's own insert rolled back (SQL Server); a writing PostgreSQL function stopped by the read-only session. A command copied across engines was not run separately (the transfer is the one entry 77 verified).
- **Not built**: change feeds with deletes, `reads:` in the dependency graph, the plan-time result-shape describe, `track_definition`, native models in the metadata documents.

## 79. Native models: `reads:`, plan-time describe, metadata

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.4): `reads:` puts a native model in the dependency graph (`graph`, selectors, `metadata_upstream`/`metadata_ancestors`) and in the build order of the models that read it; a native model without it gets a note (DDB-233, new). `plan` describes each native select it reads or copies on the engine (schema-only mode, nothing runs, `ReadSession.DescribeAsync`) and compares names and types with the declaration (DDB-230; `on_mismatch` applies); not nullability, not commands; an undescribable text is a warning. Native models are source documents with a `native` block in `metadata` (hash covers the text) and nodes of kind `native` in `graph`.
- **Verified**: unit tests (graph edges, ordering helper, the note, metadata and graph documents against the schemas); real engines (`NativeConformanceTests`): the existing native tests show no false positives (SQL Server and PostgreSQL, a PostgreSQL origin copied to SQL Server), and a native whose text returns another type or lacks a column stops the plan on both.
- **Not built**: change feeds (backlog); `track_definition` is entry 80.

## 80. `track_definition`

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.4): `track_definition: [routine, ...]` on a native model (explicit list, `schema.name`, PostgreSQL overloads with argument types). `apply` records a hash of each routine's live definition in `metadata_document` (kind `native_definition`, subject `<model>@<connection>`, so no tracking layout change); `plan` reads the live definitions (`ReadSession.RoutineDefinitionAsync`) and compares: a change is DDB-234, a warning by default (`policy.severity.native_definition_changed`), a routine with no readable definition or no tracking is DDB-235, a note. The first plan has no record and is silent.
- **Verified**: unit tests (names, the refusals, the policy default); real engines both (`NativeConformanceTests`: no warning before the first apply, DDB-235 for a missing routine, a function altered in the database gives DDB-234 and the policy makes it stop the plan, the next apply records the new definition and the warning goes).
- **Not verified**: a SQL Server login without VIEW DEFINITION (returns no definition, handled as not checked, not exercised); a definition changed between plan and apply is recorded as of the apply.
- **Not built**: change feeds (backlog).

## 81. Macros

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.5): `macros/*.sql` (DuckDB `CREATE MACRO`/`FUNCTION`/`TYPE`, several to a file; nothing else allowed), loaded on demand (only what a query reaches, callees first, types before tables before macros; cycles refused), bound by DuckDB in the lowering, `define`, `sample` and `test`. Dependencies: model to macro, macro to macro, and model to the tables of the bound plan (`ProjectContext.BaseTablesOf`) in the graph, metadata, planner ordering and selectors; the definition hash includes the reached macros' text. Lowerer: a CASE with constant conditions is folded to the branch taken, a GROUP BY key that no column determines is dropped (with `HAVING count(*) > 0` when it was the only key).
- **Verified**: unit tests (statement splitting, refusals, duplicates and cycles, on-demand prelude order, the expanded SQL of a live table and its snapshot, graph and metadata, hash, broken macro as warning, lowering off, define/sample); real engines both (`MacroConformanceTests`: one macro, a live table and its snapshot, the rendered SQL has no CASE or UNION and names only its table, the rows are the expected sums; the first run found T-SQL refusing `GROUP BY CAST(GETDATE() AS DATE)`, which the lowerer now avoids). Also checked by hand in DuckDB 1.5.4: `query_table` and `COLUMNS` with constant names, nested macros (a callee must exist first), recursion refused, several statements per file.
- **Not built**: parameters as structural constants (a `${connection.x}` that names a table or column, resolved per connection before DuckDB binds: next); column lineage through a macro (`graph --column` uses the text analyzer; a model that calls a macro has none); the macro files in a folder scope; enums mapped to engine types (not planned); `current_date` is not covered by the support matrix (DDB-305 warning).

## 82. Names as parameters

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.5): parameter type `NAME` (a table, column or other identifier; empty is `NULL`), substituted as a string constant before DuckDB binds, per connection, for macro arguments (`snapshot_at(${connection.table}, ${connection.date_col})`).
