A model whose names differ between its connections is lowered and rendered once per distinct query (`lowered.<connection>.sql`); the plan, `render`, `validate`, the graph and DDB-231 handle that (`ModelSource.QueryVariants`). The refusals: a name that is not an identifier, a parameter that is a name on some connections and a value on others, a missing value.
- **Verified**: unit tests (two connections reading different tables from one macro call, the graph and per-connection tables, the refusals, one lowering when the names agree); real engines (`MacroConformanceTests`): a live connection and a snapshot connection (two databases on SQL Server), `validate` clean, `render --check` after `render --write`, each connection's apply returns the sums of its own table.
- **Not built**: names in other positions of a query than a macro argument (a name is only ever the string a macro gets); NAME parameters in a native model's text or a copy's slice; a `NAME` in `sample`/`test` for connections other than the model's first.

## 83. Cross-connection loose ends, and column lineage through macros

- **Built**: column lineage through a macro (the analyzer reads the lowered query: `graph --column`, the metadata and `define`); the origin check also covers a copy of a model the project builds on the origin (declared by its columns; a model not built there yet is a warning, not an error); `report` shows each copy origin's last good run and latest attempt (a transfer is recorded as `from <origin>`; older records have no origin and show as an unnamed one); `plan --full-refresh <model>` reads an incremental copy from the start; `init --upgrade` brings tracking tables of layout 2 or 3 to layout 4 in place (adds `connection`, fills it with the connection initialized, new primary keys, views replaced; not on Fabric).
- **Verified**: unit tests (lineage); real engines both ways (`--full-refresh` reads a row the watermark would skip; `report` names the origin; a copy of a built model is checked: not built yet, built, then changed outside the tool; the upgrade of a layout 3 simulated from the current one on SQL Server and PostgreSQL, rows kept and the new key in force, repeatable).
- **Found**: DDB-310 cannot be satisfied by a project that builds on SQL Server and PostgreSQL together (`OPEN-ITEMS.md` M): needs a decision.
- **Not built**: see `OPEN-ITEMS.md` M.

## 84. String semantics per connection, and `--full-refresh` on any model

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 7.4): `connections.<name>.string_semantics` (the same keys as the project's; a field left out is the project's; a collation entry replaces the project's for that logical name and engine). The collation checks, the DDL (planner, origin DDL, metadata, seeds) and `check` use `ProjectConfig.ForConnection`; the header shows the overrides. A project that builds on SQL Server and PostgreSQL together sets the project's `trailing_space` and overrides it on the other engine's connections. `plan --full-refresh <model>` is accepted for any model of the project: an incremental copy is read from the start, a model every load of which rebuilds it in full gets a note (nothing to refresh), and an incremental model is pointed at `--backfill`.
- **Review**: the profile is a checked declaration, not an imposed behavior (no DuckDB-side emulation, no rewrite of comparisons on an engine that cannot match); the options for making connections agree are in `DESIGN.md` 7.4 and `OPEN-ITEMS.md` M.
- **Verified**: unit tests (merge, override of collations, the checks per connection, no DDB-310 when the connection says its own, the header, refusals of a bad value); real engines (the full-refresh note on a full model, both engines).
- **Not built**: the emulation or rewrites above; a profile per folder.

## 85. Model names out of the path: `model_layout`

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.6; note `docs/research/model-naming.md`, which also records the options and the owner's reasons): a model's name is its definition's `name:`; `model_layout` (`folder` default, `dotted`, `object`, `none`) checks the file names against it; duplicate names are an error; `define` and `import` follow the layout for new files and the definition's own file for existing ones; mapped models carry their file (`SourceDescriptor.File`) into the graph and the metadata. Two notes were written down with this: `docs/research/string-semantics-across-engines.md` (the review of what the string profile guarantees and the ways to make connections agree) and the `--full-refresh` behavior in `DESIGN.md`.
- **Verified**: unit tests (each layout, its refusal text, duplicates, an unknown value, `define` and `import` paths, the real file in the metadata). Not run on a real engine: nothing here touches one.
- **Assumption to confirm**: the default is `folder` because every existing project is laid out that way; a project that does not want folders sets another layout.
- **Not built**: the statement in the query file; a per-connection schema default.

## 86. String profile: the lint and `trimmed`

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 7.4; note `docs/research/string-semantics-across-engines.md`): DDB-236, advice per model on connections that compare strings differently (the lowered query's column uses and the declared types: `QueryFacts.ColumnUses`, `ModelLowering.StringUses`, `ProjectChecks.StringProfileAdvice`; `ProjectConfig.StringProfileDifferences`), silenced with `lint_ignore: [DDB-236]`; `trimmed: true` on a column (models and mapped models; written back by `import`), which removes the trailing-space-only case from the lint; `check` counts the rows that break it (DDB-237, `TrimmedCheck`).
- **Verified**: unit tests (a join, group, distinct, filter and order flagged only when the connections differ; trimmed and the only-trailing-space case; not flagged for integers or an order by when only trailing spaces differ; `lint_ignore`; the declaration read and written); real engines (`StringProfileConformanceTests`: SQL Server and PostgreSQL count 2 rows in the declared column, not the undeclared one, show no value, and the count clears after trimming).
- **Not built**: the DuckDB-side emulation for `sample` and `test`, the rewrite of comparisons on the engine that cannot match.

## 87. Macros: default parameters and named arguments checked

- **Checked** (no code change was needed): a macro with default parameters (`col := NULL`, `lag_days := 0`) called with arguments omitted, named, named in another order, and from inside another macro with a named argument (`snapshot_at(tbl, col := col)`). Through `render`, `validate`, `graph`, `define` and `sample` they behave as the same call written out in full: the lowered SQL is the one for that table, the graph and column lineage follow the call, and a changed default changes the model's definition hash.
- **Verified**: four unit tests (`MacroTests`: each call form, the default standing for what was left out, a named override, the hash, lineage, define and sample).
- **Noted**: a default such as `current_date - lag_days` lowers to `CAST(current_date() AS DATE) - CAST(0 AS INTEGER)` (the `DATE + INTERVAL` warning, DDB-302, applies on SQL Server); the macro's author decides that, not the tool.

## 88. The string profile in `sample` and `test`

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 7.4; note `docs/research/string-semantics-across-engines.md`): a model runs in DuckDB the way its first connection compares strings: `default_collation` from the profile's `duckdb` collation around the model's query (`SampleModel.Collation`, `ModelTestRunner`), and, where trailing spaces are ignored, the lowered query with `rtrim()` on string operands of comparisons, `IN`, `BETWEEN`, joins, window partitions, `GROUP BY` and `DISTINCT` (`PlanLowerer.Lower(..., ignoreTrailingSpaces: true)`, `ModelLowering.LowerForDuckDb`, `ProjectContext.StringEmulation`). `sample` shows what it did with the result. Also in this entry: the documents were brought up to date (`OPEN-ITEMS.md`: CI, Windows, the terminal interface, stale lines; `REVIEW.md` rewritten as one document; `DESIGN.md` stale sentences), and the repository's commit email is `dan.shryock@gmail.com` from now on.
- **Verified**: unit tests (all four combinations of case and trailing space give the group, join and filter counts the profile says; the binary expectation fails under the default profile; `sample` names what it did); real engines (`StringProfileConformanceTests`: SQL Server with a case-insensitive and a binary collation and PostgreSQL with `C` give the same counts as the emulation for the same data).
- **Not covered**: set operations without ALL, `count(DISTINCT x)` (DuckDB's `default_collation` does not reach it either), `LIKE`, native models' text. PostgreSQL's case-insensitive ICU collation was not tried (the container has no such collation configured).

## 89. `current_date` classified, and the author's table aliases come back

- **`current_date`**: the support matrix has a row for it (`fn.current_date`: native on DuckDB and PostgreSQL, emulated on SQL Server as `CAST(GETDATE() AS DATE)`), so a model that uses it gets a note (DDB-303) instead of "not covered" (DDB-305). The spike case `current_date_fn` found the real caveat: each engine reads the date in its own time zone, so with a DuckDB host in another zone than the servers the dates differ near midnight (it MISMATCHed on a Pacific-time host in the evening, and MATCHes on SQL Server 2022 and 2025 and PostgreSQL with `TZ=UTC`); the row's note says so. `current_timestamp` was already covered.
- **Table aliases** (`DESIGN.md` 7.6): `DuckParseTree.TableAliases` reads the author's tables and aliases in plan order; `PlanLowerer.Lower(..., authorAliases)` gives the n-th scan the n-th mention's alias when it is safe. `FROM staging.orders o ... EXISTS (SELECT ... FROM staging.orders q ...)` now lowers with `o`, `p` and `q` (it was `orders`, `orders_2`, `orders_3`); a mention without an alias keeps the table's name. Skipped for a CTE or a macro.
- **Verified**: unit tests (self-join aliases, an unaliased mention after an aliased one, a CTE query, the existing subquery and native tests updated to the aliases); the whole conformance suite on SQL Server and PostgreSQL, including the sample projects compared with DuckDB.
- **Not built**: author aliases for derived tables and subqueries.

## 90. A query file's head

- **Built** (`DESIGN.md` 6.5.7; note `docs/research/model-naming.md`): a query file may start with `CREATE TABLE schema.name [WITH (kind = ..., unique_key = (...), time_column = ..., lookback = '...')] AS` or `CREATE VIEW schema.name AS`. A hand-written reader (`QueryHeadParser`, DDB-238 for what it cannot read, with a line and a column) gives the model its name, table or view, and reload options; the query after `AS` is passed on unchanged. The head is a settings layer merged like the others (`kind=`: it wins over a folder default), the definition file may leave out `name:` and `kind:`, a kind said in both places, a name that differs, or `CREATE TABLE` over `kind: view` is an error, and `model_layout` applies to the head's name. `define` takes the name, kind and options from the head and writes a definition without them.
- **Verified**: unit tests (every form of the grammar, fourteen malformed heads with their positions, the project rules and conflicts, folder defaults, the layout, the line of an option error in the `.sql`, `define` with and without a key); real engines (`QueryHeadConformanceTests`: a table, a view and an incremental table declared only by their heads, built and reloaded on SQL Server and PostgreSQL).
- **Also**: the stale lines of `OPEN-ITEMS.md` were fixed (the MCP server, `import`, the web interface idea, the SQL Server 2025 CI note, a stray bullet) and the limits recorded only in this log were collected there (section O).
- **Not built**: options beyond the four, heads for native models and copies, a per-connection schema, `define` writing a head.

## 91. Terminology sweep: schema name and schema

- **Done** (owner's rule: *schema name* is the namespace, *schema* only the defined shape of a table or view): notation `schema_name.table_name` / `<schema name>` throughout the documents, diagnostics, command and option descriptions, error messages, JSON Schema descriptions and XML comments; the prose of `DESIGN.md`, `operations.md`, the skill and the research notes; the C# members that hold a schema name are `SchemaName` (`ObjectShape`, `TrackingScope`, `DuckTable`, `TrackingConfig`, `TrackingTarget`, `ConnectionTracking`, `ApplyTracking`, `DiffTable`, `ImportedObject`, `BaseTable`, the head and macro types, `ProjectConfig.TrackingSchemaName`, `ProductInfo.TrackingSchemaName`); `CatalogReader.ReadSchemaAsync` is `ReadObjectsAsync`, `PlanInput.LiveSchemas` is `LiveSchemaNames`.
- **Left as they are, by the owner's decision** (`OPEN-ITEMS.md` section Q; a short name is fine for a key or a variable, prose must say schema name): the config key `tracking.schema`, the `tracking_schema` JSON key, `diff --against-schema`, and local variables called `schema`.
- **Verified**: the unit suite passes; the conformance suite passes (no behavior changed; messages that tests assert were updated with them).

## 92. `diff` across connections

- **Built** (`CrossDiffer`, `DiffCommand`, DESIGN.md 9.10): `diff <table> --connection A --against-connection B [--against <schema_name.table_name> | --against-schema <schema name>]`. Each engine computes a digest per value from a canonical text form and only digests are compared: per bucket of key digests first, per row for the buckets that differ. Counts, the columns that differ and (with `--show-values`) samples, like the one-engine diff; the document gains `against_connection`.
- **Found by running it**: SQL Server's `CONVERT(..., 126)` leaves the fraction out when it is zero (`2000-01-01T00:00:00`), so a timestamp is written by hand with six fraction digits. `text` was treated as a type without equality on PostgreSQL too; the one-engine `diff` now treats it as an ordinary string there.
- **Verified**: unit (`CrossDiffTests`: pairing, kinds, reasons, query text); real engines both ways, SQL Server 2022 against PostgreSQL 17 with integers, decimals of different scales, text with accents and `ß`, boolean, date, timestamp, timestamp with zone, uuid, binary, time, fixed-length `char` of different lengths and NULL against the empty string (`CrossDiffConformanceTests`); an identical pair exits 0. **Not verified**: Fabric; tables of millions of rows (the digest query scans the table on each side); SQL Server 2025.
- **Limits**: floating point is not compared; a PostgreSQL `numeric` without precision is not compared; there is no row filter.

## 93. Getting started and a generated command reference

- **Built**: `docs/getting-started.md` (offline tour with the `starter` template, project layout, the two logins, `init`, `plan`, `apply`, `run`) and `docs/commands.md`, generated by `CommandReference.Render()` from the command tree the program runs; `CommandReferenceTests` compares the file and `UPDATE_GOLDEN=1` rewrites it, so a new command or option cannot go undocumented. The offline steps of the tour were run on the `starter` template (validate, seed, sample, test, render, loads); the database steps follow `operations.md` and the conformance tests and were not re-run for this entry.

## 94. Fuzzing of the other inputs a person edits

- **Built** (`FuzzTests`): seeded mutations (25 per file kind, the same operators as before) of a hook script, a macro file, native query text, a native model's definition, a metadata test, a model test and a query file's head, each run through `validate`, `test`, `loads`, `metadata` and `define --check`, which must finish without an internal error (DDB-900) and with a well-formed JSON document. All passed on the first run, so no defect was found by it. Not covered: the interactive question flow (it needs a terminal) and the resolver query results.

## 95. `ANY`, `ALL` and row-value `IN` used as a value

- **Built** (`PlanLowerer.MarksAsValues`, `AsValueCondition`; DESIGN.md 7.6): a comparison mark that a filter does not consume is written as `(CASE WHEN EXISTS (S WHERE p) THEN TRUE WHEN NOT EXISTS (S WHERE p OR an operand IS NULL) THEN FALSE ELSE NULL END)` where it appears (select list, `ORDER BY`, `GROUP BY`, aggregates, derived tables, inside `CASE` and `IS NULL`, and inside conditions that are not AND/OR/NOT of marks). It was refused (DDB-324) before.
- **Verified**: 12 unit forms against DuckDB's own rows on data with NULLs on both sides, correlated and not, through a derived table and a `GROUP BY` of the value; on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 five value forms (ANY, ALL, `<> ALL` with a filter, `NOT ANY`, row-value `NOT IN`) give DuckDB's TRUE, FALSE and NULL for each row. **Not verified**: a mark carried out of the query level its predicates belong to is not detected by the lowerer (the unit forms through a derived table pass); DuckDB 2.0's different plan shape (see section A item 3).

## 96. An aggregate (or a GROUP BY key) over a subquery

- **Built** (`PlanLowerer`, the aggregate node; DESIGN.md 7.6): when an aggregate's argument or a group key holds a subquery, the child is wrapped in a derived table first, so the subquery is a column of the derived table and the aggregate reads the column. SQL Server answers `sum((SELECT ...))` with "Cannot perform an aggregate function on an expression containing an aggregate or a subquery"; PostgreSQL accepts it either way.
- **Verified**: three unit forms equal to DuckDB's rows (a correlated count inside `sum`, a `max` over `coalesce` of a subquery, a `GROUP BY` with a sum over a subquery); on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 the two model forms give 5 and 7|8; the SQL Server test fails without the change.

## 97. `changed:` follows the files a model depends on

- **Built** (`ProjectContext.ChangedThroughOtherFiles`; DESIGN.md 9.9 selectors): besides a model's own `.sql` and `.yml`, a changed source descriptor and `dbdatabuild.yml`, `changed:<ref>` now selects the models beneath a changed `_dbdatabuild.yml`, the readers of a changed `.native.sql`, the models that run a changed hook script (groups expanded, per connection), the models whose queries reach a macro of a changed `macros/` file (the closure: macros that call macros), and the model a changed `rendered/` file belongs to.
- **Verified**: `GraphTests` (a git repository: touch a hook script, a macro file, a folder file, a rendered file; each selects exactly the models it should). The native-text branch has no test of its own (it reuses the descriptor lookup).

## 98. Slice lint: the source column's index (DDB-239)

- **Built** (`ModelLowering.SourceColumnsWithoutLeadingIndex`, `ProjectChecks.SliceSourceAdvice`; DESIGN.md 6.6.2): for a `watermark_append` or `delete_insert_by_range` load (including the implicit one of `incremental_by_time_range`), the slice column is traced by lineage to a mapped model's column, and a warning is given when the project declares indexes or a grain for that source and none leads with the column. The diagnostic, its fixture entry, the `lint_ignore` enum and the config text are in place; `lint: { slices: false }` silences it too.
- **Verified**: unit only (`SliceAdviceTests`: the warning, a declared index, a grain that starts with the column, a source with nothing declared, a computed column, the two silences). It reads declarations, not a database, so no engine is involved; whether a real imported descriptor lists the indexes a DBA expects is the `import` check (entry 36).

## 99. A test gate for `plan`, `check` and `run`

- **Built** (`ProjectConfig.TestGateTags`, `PlanningSession.Prepare`; DESIGN.md 9.8): `tests: { gate: { tags: [...] } }`. After the project validates and before the target is read (so no login is needed to learn the gate failed), the tests with those tags run through the same code as `dbdatabuild test --tag`; a failed or errored test refuses the plan with exit 1 and prints the tests; a pass prints one line. Loader diagnostics for a damaged setting; `config.schema.json` has the key.
- **Not built**: a severity floor, recording the result in the plan and its hash (the plan file format and the hash would change; not done without the owner), gating `apply`.
- **Verified**: unit (`TestGateTests`: a failing gated test refuses `plan` and `check` before the target is touched, a passing gate is reported and planning goes on to the target, another tag does not gate, no gate runs nothing, five damaged settings). No real engine is involved (the gate runs before the target is read).

## 100. History inconsistency reaches downstream models (DDB-240)

- **Built** (`HistoryConcerns`, `PlanningSession`, `policy.severity.history_inconsistency`; DESIGN.md 12.3): `plan`, `check` and `run` read the column history with the snapshot (`HistoryReader`), follow column lineage from each unacknowledged DDB-443 entry to the columns built from it, through any number of models, and report each model about to be loaded that has such a column. A warning by default; `error` refuses the plan before anything is written ("Nothing was planned"), and an acknowledgement (`ack history`) clears it. The model that owns the column is not reported against itself.
- **Verified**: unit (`HistoryConcernsTests`: a direct and a chained reader, the owner, a non-reader and an acknowledged entry are quiet, the policy sets the severity); SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 (`A_history_warning_can_be_accepted_without_touching_data`: a view over the new column gets DDB-240 in `plan`, the policy at `error` refuses it, and after `ack history` the plan is quiet). Limits: lineage is by the model's own query (a macro is followed through the lowered query, a native model's text is not); the history read is one more set of four queries per plan on a tracked connection.

## 101. Model tags and the `tag:` selector

- **Built** (`ModelDefinition.Tags`, `ModelDefinitionLoader`, `ProjectContext.ResolveCore`): `tags: [a, b]` in a model's definition or in a `defaults:` section above it (a layered key: lists append, `tags=` replaces, `tags-` removes), a format check (letters, digits, `_`, `-`, `.`, `:`), `tag:<name>` in every model selector (and in the `--help` text and `docs/commands.md`), `tags` in the model metadata document (omitted when there are none, so existing documents and hashes do not change), and the key in the JSON Schemas (`model`, `source` for the layered `defaults:` of a folder, `config`, `metadata`). A mapped or native model accepts the key (a folder's `defaults: tags` reaches it) and does not use it.
- **Fixed on the way**: the selector table in `DESIGN.md` still said `target:` for `connection:`.
- **Verified**: unit (`ModelTagTests`: merging down the project files, `tags=` and `tags-`, the intersection with another selector term, the metadata document, an unknown tag as a usage error, a malformed tag). Nothing in the tool acts on a tag yet except selection: the test gate (entry 99) is about test tags, a different thing.

## 102. Two real applies at once, a killed connection, and what `report` calls unfinished

- **Verified** (`ApplyConformanceTests`, SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17): (1) the first of two plans waits in a hook (`WAITFOR DELAY` / `pg_sleep`) while a second `apply` of another plan starts: the second is refused with DDB-439 and changes nothing, the first completes, and the refused plan applies once the lock is free. (2) The session of an `apply` is killed (`KILL` / `pg_terminate_backend`) inside a hook: the apply ends with exit 1 and DDB-440, no DDB-900 and no crash; the lock went with the session, so `apply --resume` runs at once and finishes the plan.
- **Found**: a step cut off by a lost connection is left `started` in `run_log` (nothing can write the outcome), and `report` kept listing "load of ... did not finish ok" under *Needs attention* for ever, even after the resume ran the step again and it finished; the same held for a step that had failed and was resumed. **Changed** (`ReportCommand`): a `run_log` or `ddl_log` row that did not end `ok` is no longer listed when the same step of the same plan (same `step_id`; for DDL the same object and statement hash) has a later row that ended `ok`. The rows themselves are untouched, so the history still shows the interruption.
- **Not tested**: a half-open connection (a network partition with no reset), a server restart, many applies at once.

## 103. A read login without VIEW DEFINITION; copies under other time zones

- **Verified on SQL Server 2022** (`NativeConformanceTests`): a read login with `SELECT, EXECUTE` on the schemas and no `VIEW DEFINITION` plans normally (exit 0); a routine in `track_definition` is reported as not checked (DDB-235, "no permission to see it") and is **not** reported as changed (no DDB-234).
- **Found**: with an explicit `DENY VIEW DEFINITION` the login cannot see the tracking tables in the catalog either, and `plan` stopped with DDB-505 "the tracking tables do not exist ... Run `init`", which sends the person the wrong way. The DDB-505 fix and explanation now say that the read login needs SELECT on the tracking schema name and that a DENY hides the tables. The behaviour is unchanged (the tool cannot tell a hidden table from a missing one with that login).
- **Verified**: the copy tests (both directions, all value types) and the cross-connection `diff` test pass with the host's time zone set to `America/Los_Angeles` (DST) and `Asia/Kolkata` (a half-hour offset) (`TZ=... dotnet test`). Not varied: the database server's own time zone setting.

## 104. A native command copied across engines

- **Verified on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17, both directions** (`NativeConformanceTests`): a native command (a procedure on SQL Server, a function on PostgreSQL) on one connection is the origin of a copy on the other. The plan has a transfer step marked `command: true`; planning never calls the routine; `apply` without `--allow-risky` is refused; with it the origin runs the call in a transaction that is rolled back (the routine's side effect is not kept), and the rows arrive on the destination with their values.

## 105. Cross-connection `diff` at scale, and an unhandled driver error that was not scrubbed

- **Measured** (`CrossDiffScaleTests`, off unless `DDB_SCALE=1`, row count `DDB_SCALE_ROWS`): SQL Server 2022 against PostgreSQL 17, five columns (a key, an integer, a decimal, text, a timestamp), five rows changed, one missing and one extra: 1,000,000 rows in 25.8 s, 5,000,000 rows in 126 s (108 s once the two sides are asked at once). The differences were all found. Counting distinct keys inside the bucket query to save the duplicate-key scan was tried and was slower (161 s), so it was not kept.
- **Found at 5,000,000 rows**: (1) the read session's queries had the driver's default 30-second command timeout, and `diff` failed with "Execution Timeout Expired". `ReadSession.CommandTimeoutSeconds` is new; `diff` sets it to 0 (no limit; the person can interrupt). Other read queries keep the default. (2) That failure was printed as System.CommandLine's default **"Unhandled exception: Microsoft.Data.SqlClient.SqlException ..." with the driver's message and a stack trace**: the library's own handler caught it before the tool's guard, so the rule that driver messages are never echoed (DESIGN.md 14.2) did not hold for an exception that a command does not catch. `EnableDefaultExceptionHandler` is now off, so the guard's scrubbed internal error (the exception's type) is what the person sees; `UnhandledDriverErrorTests` fails without the change.
- **Not measured**: wider tables, text-heavy columns, tables with a poorly selective key.

## 106. A statement of `apply` had the drivers' 30-second timeout

- **Found** by going looking for other default timeouts after entry 105: every statement `apply` ran through the gate (`MutationGate.ExecuteAsync`: DDL, loads, hooks, index creation) had the driver's default command timeout, 30 seconds on both Microsoft.Data.SqlClient and Npgsql. The load of a large model, a `full_replace` of millions of rows or an index build that took longer failed with a timeout and the step was recorded as failed. The conformance tests never ran a statement that long.
- **Fixed**: statements have no time limit (`CommandTimeout = 0`): a started statement is never abandoned for being slow, which is the rule the executor already follows for everything else (the person can stop between steps, DESIGN.md 10.4). Reads that scan a table, a resolver, a range's bounds and a copy's newest value, now get ten minutes (`TargetSnapshotReader.ScanTimeoutSeconds`), and the trimmed-column count of `check` thirty; catalog and tracking reads keep the driver's default, so a read blocked behind a lock still ends. `diff` has no limit (entry 105).
- **Verified**: a hook that waits 32 seconds (`WAITFOR DELAY` / `pg_sleep`) in an `apply` fails on both engines without the change and passes with it (`A_statement_that_runs_longer_than_the_drivers_default_timeout_is_not_abandoned`, a minute of the suite).

## 107. A project of hundreds of models: validation grew with the square of the models

- **Measured** (a generated project: 10 mapped tables and 100 or 300 models, each a join of two earlier tables, Debug build): `validate` took 9.1 s for 100 models and 59 s for 300; `render --write` 60 s; `metadata` 62 s. The time per model rose from 0.09 s to 0.2 s: not linear.
- **Cause**: for each model DuckDB was given an empty copy of *every* declared table of the project (a `CREATE TABLE` per model and mapped table, repeated for the plan, the describe and the lowering), so the work grew with models times tables.
- **Fixed** (`ModelLowering.UpstreamFor`): DuckDB is given only the declared tables the query's text names (found by the parser); every table when the query reaches a macro (a macro can name a table through an argument) or its text cannot be read. A refusal is never given on the restricted set: when the quick way fails, the lowering is computed again on every table and that answer is the one reported, so a table the quick way missed cannot cause a refusal.
- **After**: `validate` 3.3 s for 100 models and 8.8 s for 300; `render --check` 8.5 s; `metadata` 11.6 s; `loads` 3.3 s; `graph --columns` 2 s. Output is byte-identical (the unit suite, with its goldens, passes). **Not done**: `plan` on such a project (it needs a database; the planner's own cost was not measured), and a release build (the numbers are Debug).

## 108. `apply` re-read the schemas of every object under the schema name after every step

- **Measured** (the generated project of entry 107, 300 models in one schema name on PostgreSQL 17, 401 steps): `apply` took 199 s, 0.5 s a step.
- **Cause**: after each DDL step (and in the staleness check of each base object) the executor read the live shape of the object it had just touched through `CatalogReader.ReadObjectsAsync`, which read **every** table and view of the schema name (two catalog queries, about 1,200 rows) and then picked one: the cost of a step grew with the objects in the schema name, and an apply with the square of them.
- **Fixed**: `ReadObjectsAsync` takes an optional object name and the four catalog queries filter on it (`@object`); the executor asks for the one object. Planning still reads each schema name once.
- **After**: the same plan applies in 9 s (a twentieth). A plan, `plan` again after the apply and `report` take 12 s and 1.5 s; the plan's own time is the offline checks of entry 107.

## 109. More probes of size, and lineage against the tables a query names

- **Probed** (Debug build, generated projects): a model of 600 columns validates, renders and passes `define --check` in about a second; a chain of 1,500 views each reading the previous one validates in 38 s, and `graph` follows it from either end in under a second (no recursion limit is hit); a project of 300 models in one schema name plans in 12 s and applies 401 steps in 13 s on SQL Server 2022 (9 s on PostgreSQL 17, entry 108).
- **Fixed** (`MetadataBuilder.AnalyzeAgainstUpstream`): lineage and nullability analysis (`metadata`, `graph --columns`, string-use lint) was given the declared columns of every table of the project for each model, as lowering had been (entry 107); it is now given those of the tables the query names. `metadata` for 300 models: 11.6 s to 9.3 s. Output unchanged (the unit suite passes).
- **Left**: `metadata` still costs about 30 ms a model (1,500 models: 95 s before this change); most of it is DuckDB binding each query.

## 110. Names with keywords, spaces, accents and quotes; names too long for an engine (DDB-241)

- **Verified on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17** (`Reserved_words_spaces_accents_and_quotes_in_names_are_quoted_on_both_engines`): columns named `order`, `group`, `my col`, `Café` and `a"b` in a mapped table, a model named `rpt.order` that reads them, renames them to `select` and `from`, declares them in its unique key and in an index, loads twice (the second time incrementally) and reports clean: every name is quoted for each engine, in DDL, loads, indexes and the catalog comparison. No defect found.
- **Found** (`A_name_longer_than_the_engine_keeps_is_refused_before_anything_is_planned`): a 70-character column name passed `validate` and `plan` on PostgreSQL, which cuts an identifier at 63 bytes without a word; the table it built had a shorter column name than the declaration, the step failed its post-condition (DDB-441), and the next `plan` stopped with a question that had nothing to do with the cause (DDB-414).
- **Built**: DDB-241, an error from `validate` and `plan`: for each connection a model is built on, its schema name, object name, column names and index names are checked against that engine's limit (63 bytes on PostgreSQL, 128 characters on SQL Server and Fabric); the message names the name, the length and the connection. A model built on both engines is checked against each. Unit tests (`NameLengthTests`: 63/64 bytes, accented text counted in bytes, 128/129 characters, two connections); on SQL Server a 70-character name applies as before.
- **Not covered**: names the tool makes itself are shortened already (the staging table of a copy, advised indexes); the tracking schema name and the names of parameters are not checked.

## 111. Incremental load by key at two million rows

- **Measured** (`An_incremental_load_by_key_of_two_million_rows_takes_seconds_not_minutes`, off unless `DDB_SCALE=1`): a source of 2,000,000 rows, a model `incremental_by_unique_key` on the key, local containers. First load: 6.1 s on SQL Server 2022, 3.6 s on PostgreSQL 17. After changing 10% of the source rows, the second load (delete the keys, insert them again): 10.3 s and 4.5 s; the row count stays exact. Includes the plan. No defect found.

## 112. `report` on an object whose model left the project

- **Found** (a 300-model project on PostgreSQL, two models deleted after the apply): `plan` was silent, the objects stayed on the target (the tool never drops one, by design), and `report` listed them as "in sync", which says nothing about the fact that no model builds them any more.
- **Changed** (`ReportCommand`): an object the tool has recorded whose name is not a model of the project now reads `in sync (no model in the project; the tool never drops it)`. The `now` field of the JSON document carries the same text. No new finding and no change to `plan`: what to do with such an object (drop it by hand, keep it, build it again) is the person's decision.
- **Verified**: SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 (`A_model_that_left_the_project_stays_on_the_target_and_the_report_says_nothing_builds_it_any_more`: the view is still there after the next plan, nothing is planned for it, and only that object is labelled).

## 113. A refused login is not "report this to the maintainer"

- **Found** while checking entry 105's fix by hand: a wrong password reached the top-level guard, which reported DDB-900 ("The tool failed with ... Report this with the command line used"), as if the tool had a bug.
- **Changed** (`CliApp.Guarded`, DDB-242): a database exception that no command handled is reported as DDB-242, exit 1, not DDB-900, exit 70. The text carries the exception's type and the driver's error number (`SqlException (error 18456)`, `PostgresException (error 28P01)`: a failed login on each engine, checked against both containers), never the driver's message. The diagnostic's fix lists the numbers that mean a refused login or a closed connection. In JSON mode the document has the diagnostic. Other exceptions are still DDB-900.
- **Verified**: unit (`UnhandledDriverErrorTests`: text and JSON, no server name and no password in either); by hand on both containers with a wrong password.

## 114. Cross-connection `diff` when most rows differ

- **Found by reading my own design**: the second level of `diff --against-connection` fetches the digest of every row of the buckets that differ. When a column differs systematically between two engines (the likeliest reason to compare them), every bucket differs and the fetch is every row: 5 million rows of six digests is more than a gigabyte of strings.
- **Built** (`CrossDiffer.MaxDrillRows`, `PartialDiff`): when the buckets that differ hold more than 1,000,000 rows (the larger side), the comparison stops after the first level and says so: the JSON has `rows.partial` (`buckets_differing`, `buckets`, `rows_in_differing_buckets`, `columns_differing`; `identical` is false, the row counts are not known), and the text names the columns whose values differ in some bucket and suggests `--exclude-columns` or `--columns` to look at the rest row by row.
- **Verified** (SQL Server 2022 against PostgreSQL 17): with the limit lowered to 1,000 and every one of 5,000 rows different in one column, the result is partial and names that column; leaving it out with `--exclude-columns` compares the other columns row by row and finds them identical (exit 0). The limit itself (1,000,000) was not exercised on a table that large.
- **Also seen under Wine** (`scripts/test-windows-wine.sh`, unit suite, 1,786 run): 5 failures, none in code touched by this work: two tests need `git`, which is not in the Wine prefix (the old `changed:` test and the new one), two web-server tests (a symbolic link and a loopback request, Wine's semantics), and the agent-kit schema comparison, which failed only because a schema was edited while the run was in progress. Windows itself passes in CI on every push.

## 115. The history read takes only the plans that hold a history answer

- **Found** while checking the cost of entry 100: `plan` now reads the column history on every run, and `HistoryReader` read the full text of every completed plan to look for answers (`Q-history-...`). A plan of 400 steps is hundreds of kilobytes, so the read grew with the number of applies the connection has ever had.
- **Changed** (`HistoryReader`): the query keeps only plans whose text contains `Q-history-` (`LIKE` on the plan text, on both engines). The history report and DDB-240 are built from those answers and nothing else, so what they say does not change. A plan that cannot be read back is now only reported when its text still holds a history answer.
- **Verified**: the history, `run` and JSON-command conformance tests on both engines pass unchanged.

## 116. `plan` timed out once the tracking log tables held a few hundred thousand rows (PostgreSQL)

- **Found** by filling the tracking tables the way a year of scheduled runs would (`run_log` and `ddl_log`, 200,000 rows each, synthetic): on PostgreSQL 17 `plan` took 48 s and then failed with a driver timeout (reported as DDB-242). The snapshot read the newest `ok` row of each object and model with `... = (SELECT MAX(...) FROM same_table x WHERE x.object = d.object)`, a correlated subquery that PostgreSQL runs once per row, and neither log table has an index on the columns it matches. The work grew with the square of the log.
- **Fixed** (`TargetSnapshotReader.ReadAsync`): both reads use `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY object ORDER BY time DESC)` and keep the first row: one pass, and exactly one row per object even when two rows share a timestamp (the old query could return both, and building the dictionary from them would have thrown). `schema_version` keeps its correlated form: its primary key is `(connection, object_name, first_seen_utc)`, so the subquery is an index seek.
- **After**: with 4,000,000 rows in the two tables on PostgreSQL, `plan` takes 14.6 s (13 s with almost no history), `check` 14.7 s and `report` 4.3 s; with 2,000,000 rows on SQL Server 2022, 14 s, 12.8 s and 1.8 s. SQL Server's optimizer had coped with the old form; the new one is the same on both.
- **Not measured**: the same history with many connections sharing one tracking schema name; a tracking schema name on another server (the reads then cross a network).

## 117. The history read asks only about the models that have a history answer

- **Changed** (`HistoryReader`): after the plans that hold a history answer are read (entry 115), the `schema_version` and `operation_interval` reads are limited to the models those answers name (up to 1,000, as parameters), and nothing more is read when there are none. A connection's `operation_interval` has a row per range of every load, and `plan` reads the history on every run (entry 100), so the read grew with the connection's age for models that never had a column added. What the history report and DDB-240 say does not change.
- **Verified**: the history, `run` and JSON-command tests on both engines pass; the full non-release conformance run (167 run, 5 skipped) passed before this change and these tests after it.

## 118. What a scheduled `run` leaves on disk

- **Measured** (the 300-model PostgreSQL project, a run of 100 loads): a plan file 170 KB, its report 74 KB, a statement log about 2 MB. A job every 15 minutes leaves hundreds of megabytes a month; nothing prunes them (the plan is also kept in `migration_log`).
- **Done**: `docs/operations.md` section 4 says so and gives a `find ... -delete` line; `OPEN-ITEMS.md` section D0 lists a retention setting as a decision for the owner. No behaviour changed.

## 119. A copy of three million rows

- **Measured** (`A_table_of_a_few_hundred_thousand_rows_moves_in_bulk` with `DDB_SCALE=1 DDB_SCALE_ROWS=3000000`; SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 in local containers; a table of eleven columns with text, decimals and timestamps): 3,000,000 rows in 59.0 s from PostgreSQL to SQL Server and 58.8 s the other way, about 50,000 rows a second, with the peak working set of the whole test process at 225 to 232 MB (so the transfer holds one batch at a time, not the table). Row counts and a column sum agree. The default stays 300,000 rows.
- **Found in the test harness only**: the helper that fills the source table had the driver's 30-second command timeout (`Engine.ExecAsync`); it now has none.

## 120. Non-ASCII text literals were lost on SQL Server

- **Found** by a probe with a literal of mixed text (`'café ☕ 😀 日本語 it''s a \ b '` and a newline) in a model: on PostgreSQL the row held it exactly; on SQL Server it held `café ? ?? ???`. The rendered T-SQL wrote the literal as `'...'`, which SQL Server reads as `varchar` in the database's code page, and only then converts it to the `nvarchar` column the tool creates: every character outside the code page became `?`, silently. It applied to loads and to views.
- **Fixed** (`TsqlLiterals.Nationalize`, called from `TargetRules.Finish` for `sqlserver`): a literal with a character above U+007F is written `N'...'`. The scan keeps ASCII literals, comments (line and nested block), `[quoted]` and `"quoted"` identifiers and a literal that already has its `N` as they were, and does not loop on an unterminated literal. A project that has such a literal sees `render --check` report the file out of date once; the fix is `render --write`. Not applied to Fabric (unverified) or to hook scripts.
- **Verified**: 12 unit cases; SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 give the same text back from a table load and from a view (`Text_literals_with_emoji_cjk_quotes_backslashes_and_newlines_survive_a_load_on_both_engines`).

## 121. A decimal constant wider than 18 digits could not be lowered

- **Found** by a probe that selects a constant of each type (`Constants_of_every_type_come_back_exactly_from_a_load_on_both_engines`): `CAST(123456789012345678901234567890.123456 AS DECIMAL(36, 6))` stopped `render` with DDB-324 ("a plan shape the lowerer does not handle (FormatException)"). DuckDB keeps a decimal wider than 18 digits (and a `HUGEINT`) in 128 bits, and the plan carries it as `{"upper": signed 64 bits, "lower": unsigned 64 bits}` instead of a number; the lowerer read every constant with `BigInteger.Parse`.
- **Fixed** (`PlanLowerer.IntegerValue`): the value is `upper * 2^64 + lower`; used for decimal constants and for integer constants of a wide type. Four unit cases (30 digits, negative with 38, 24 decimal places, 38 nines) equal DuckDB's own text for the lowered query.
- **Verified**: a model of constants on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 returns a timestamp with microseconds, the first and last date, a 36-digit decimal, a small negative decimal, the largest and smallest `BIGINT`, both booleans, a typed NULL, the empty string, two spaces (kept, length 2), a uuid and a `DOUBLE` of 1.5e300 exactly as DuckDB has them. Nothing else in that list failed.

## 122. A copy of a zoned timestamp from SQL Server to PostgreSQL failed

- **Found** by copying the types the main copy test does not carry (`Binary_uuid_time_and_zoned_timestamp_values_copy_exactly_and_the_diff_agrees`): from SQL Server, a `datetimeoffset` with an offset of +02:00 stopped the transfer step (InvalidOperationException, reported as DDB-440). Npgsql writes a `timestamptz` only from an offset of zero, and the transfer passed the value through with the offset it had.
- **Fixed** (`Transfer.Convert`): a `TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE` value is converted to UTC, which keeps the instant (SQL Server accepts any offset). The offset a value was written with is not carried: it was never a part of what PostgreSQL can hold.
- **Verified**: both directions on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17, four rows each (a binary of 3 bytes, an empty one, a NULL row and a 200,000-byte one; uuids from all zeros to all ones; times down to the microsecond and 23:59:59.999999; zoned timestamps with an offset, at UTC, and at 9999-12-31): the rows arrive and `diff --against-connection` of the origin table against the destination table is identical (the diff converts to UTC, so it also shows the instants agree). The check uses the tool's own cross-connection diff on its own output.

## 123. Text edge values in a copy

- **Verified** (`Text_with_edge_values_copies_exactly_and_the_diff_agrees`, SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17, both directions): trailing and leading spaces, the empty string against NULL, a tab, `\r\n`, `\n` and a lone `\n`, a combining accent beside the composed one, an emoji and a flag (surrogate pairs), Hebrew and Arabic, a sharp s, a dotted capital I, a final sigma, an angstrom sign, a zero-width space, a no-break space, a line separator, quotes and a backslash, and 3,999 characters, in a column of limited length and an unlimited one. Every value arrives byte for byte: `diff --against-connection` of the origin table against the destination table is identical, and the diff's text digest is exact (case, trailing spaces and normalization forms all count). No defect found.

## 124. A byte order mark at the start of a file

- **Probed** (the `starter` template converted to CRLF line endings, then with a UTF-8 byte order mark on every `.sql` and `.yml`, the way an editor on Windows saves them): CRLF changes nothing (`validate`, `render --check`, `define --check`, `test` and `sample` all agree with the LF project and the rendered hashes are the same). A mark: `validate`, `render --check`, `test` and `sample` were fine, but `define --check` reported "Unknown key `\uFEFFname`" (DDB-104 and DDB-105) for every definition, and a query file's head would not have been recognised as one: `DefineCommand` reads a file as bytes (to hash it) and `Encoding.GetString` keeps the mark as a character, while `File.ReadAllText` drops it.
- **Fixed** (`DefineCommand.Utf8Text`): the mark is skipped when a definition or a query file is turned into text. `define --write` rewrites a marked definition without it (files are written as UTF-8 without a mark, CLAUDE.md); the hash check is on the original bytes.
- **Verified**: `ByteOrderMarkTests` (a project with and without marks gives the same exit code for `validate`, `define --check` and `render --check`; a query file with a head and a mark still has its head); two of the four cases fail without the change.

## 125. Ctrl-C during an apply

- **Found** while reading how the CLI stops: the rule "a statement that has started is never abandoned" held for the terminal and web interfaces (a stop flag the apply loop checks between steps) but not for the command line, where Ctrl-C ended the process in the middle of a statement and left a `started` step with no outcome.
- **Changed** (`ApplyCommand`): when `apply` (and so `run`) is not hosted by an interface with its own stop, the first Ctrl-C sets the stop flag and prints a line ("the apply stops after the step that is running; press Ctrl-C again to end it now"); the apply loop stops before the next step (DDB-445 "Apply was stopped by the operator", exit 1) and the plan is resumable. The second Ctrl-C is the default: the process ends. A dry run keeps the default behaviour.
- **Verified** (SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17, Linux, the real executable in its own process with SIGINT): during a 6-second hook the signal is sent; the apply finishes the hook, runs nothing after it, exits 1 with DDB-445, and `apply --resume` completes the plan. The test starts `dotnet dbdatabuild.dll` from the CLI project's own output folder, because the conformance output folder lacks some of the libraries the CLI loads (a `FileNotFoundException` on PostgreSQL when it was tried there).
- **Not verified**: Windows (Ctrl-C there is the same event in .NET; no test).

## 126. `current_timestamp` and `now()`

- **Found** by writing a model that stamps its rows (`SELECT ..., now() AS loaded_at, current_timestamp AS t2, current_date AS d`) and reading the rendered text: (1) `current_timestamp` is bound by DuckDB as `get_current_timestamp()`, which the lowered query kept and neither engine has, so a model that used it failed at `apply` on both SQL Server and PostgreSQL; (2) `now()` became `GETDATE()` on SQL Server, the server's local clock *without a zone*, which a `TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE` column reads as UTC: right only on a server at UTC, wrong by the offset on any other (DuckDB's `now()` is a point in time).
- **Fixed**: (1) the lowerer writes `get_current_timestamp()` as `now()` (the same value, and a function the transpile knows). (2) a new target rule `now-with-zone` for SQL Server writes `now()` as `SYSDATETIMEOFFSET()` (marker `ddb_sysdatetimeoffset`, replaced in `TargetRules.Finish`); it is in `RewriteCatalog`, the JSON Schemas' rule lists and can be turned off like the others (`rewrites: {disable: [now-with-zone]}`). `current_date`, `today()`, `localtimestamp` and `current_time` stay on the local clock (`GETDATE()`, cast), as before.
- **Verified**: unit (`TargetRulesTests`: the rule fires on SQL Server and not on PostgreSQL, a cast of it, `date_trunc` of it); SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 load `now()` and `current_timestamp` into zoned columns within five minutes of the engine's own clock (`The_current_time_is_loaded_as_the_instant_it_is_on_both_engines`). **Not verified**: the rule on Fabric (not applied: unverified).

## 127. The whole real-engine suite against servers that are not at UTC and not in English

- **Done** (throwaway containers, removed afterwards): (1) SQL Server 2022 with `TZ=America/Los_Angeles` (the server's clock at -07:00; on that server the old `GETDATE()` text, read as UTC, was 420 minutes from `SYSUTCDATETIME()`, and the new text is not) and the test process also in Los Angeles: the full non-release conformance suite, 179 tests run, all pass. (2) The same server with the `sa` login's default language set to German (session `DATEFORMAT` dmy, where `CAST('2024-02-03' AS datetime)` is 2 March): 179 run, all pass; the tool writes dates as `date` and `datetime2`, whose ISO text does not depend on the language. (3) PostgreSQL 17 with `TZ`/`PGTZ=Asia/Kolkata` (+05:30) and the test process in Kolkata: 178 of 179 pass; the one that did not is `upper('ß')` in the engine-difference probes, which differs because the container image I used (`postgres:17`, Debian, glibc) is not the one the suite normally runs on (`postgres:17-alpine`), not because of the time zone.
- **So**: the zone rule, the copy of zoned timestamps, the cross-connection digests (timestamps are canonical text), the loads of dates and times and the weekday rule hold on a server whose clock, language and first day of the week are not the defaults of the test containers.

## 128. Open items cleaned up; why the MCP app did not appear

- **Cleaned** (`OPEN-ITEMS.md`): the header counts and date; stale or garbled lines in A (an enumeration of lowering gaps that had rules since, now a pointer to the research notes), D (the lone "corrected in this commit", D0 folded into D), E (refreshed order), G (the parameter type check exists; the slice lint does look at source indexes, DDB-239), I (`import-sources`), K (a garbled `diff` line, `.mcpb` packaging that exists, web and MCP-app items that are built), O (the parameter types wording); first-person wording removed; sections H, I, J put back in order. The owner's trial of the skill and the MCP server from Windows is recorded (works at some level, not thorough).
- **The MCP app**: the owner could not make Claude Code in the Claude Desktop app on Windows use it. By design the server offers the app and the `show` tool only to a host that sends the `io.modelcontextprotocol/ui` capability, and says nothing when it does not. `dbdatabuild mcp` now writes one line to standard error at connect (client, whether MCP Apps and elicitation are supported), and `docs/interfaces.md` says to look for it. Whether that host advertises the extension is not known here; no real host has been used with the app (only a stand-in and the extension's reference host).

## 129. `show` for a host without the MCP app: a one-time link

- **Why**: on 0.3.1 the owner's session in Claude Code (Claude Desktop, Windows) answered "not through this server" when asked for a screen: `show` was offered only to hosts that advertise MCP Apps, and the skill describes it as if it were always there.
- **Built** (`McpServer.ShowByLink`, `WebServer` one-time links): `show` is always listed. For a host without the app it starts the web page on the loopback address (once per server, read-only; `--allow-apply` as for the app), and the result is a link `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/?token=...&screen=<screen>` that works once and for ten minutes. Opening it spends the link, sets a session cookie and redirects to the address without the token; the page keeps the session token for its calls; the API accepts only a session token (a link is not one). The first screen is the one asked for. `dbdatabuild web` is unchanged. The server's connect line on standard error and the skill now say what `show` does in each kind of host.
- **Limit, stated plainly**: the link is in the model's hands before it is in the person's, so whoever opens it first gets the session. The model is told not to; a model that can run commands could. The one-time rule keeps the link's text from working later, in a transcript or a log; it does not make the model unable to look at the page. Everything the page can do is what the read-only commands and (with `--allow-apply`) the person's Apply button can do; `--show-values` and sample data are in the page as they are in `dbdatabuild web`, so the model-sees-no-data rule rests on the model not using the link.
- **Switch** (added after review): `dbdatabuild mcp --no-show` removes the `show` tool for every host. The owner's rule for this: the defaults are guardrails that keep the agent doing the right thing, real barriers are the logins and the `--allow-*` options, and a reasonable simple scenario gets no extra guards (so no confirmation step on the link).
- **Verified**: unit (`OneTimeLinkTests`: the redirect, the cookie, the page and the API with the session, the spent link, a link used as a session token, a stranger, two sessions; `McpServerTests`: `show` in a host without the app returns the link, which opens once). Not verified: in a real host, and on Windows.

## 130. A walk through plan, apply, run and report (a review, nothing changed)

- **Method**: the `starter` project on SQL Server 2022: `load-seeds`, `init`, `plan` (before and after `render --write`), `apply`, `plan`, `run`, `run` again, `check`, `report`, a comment added to a model and `run`; the plan report, the apply output and the statement log read.
- **Found**: (1) a plan's id is the date plus a hash of its content, and a plan is applied once, so `run` with unchanged content fails with DDB-438, exit 1, from its second run of the day; (2) the rows of a load in `run_log` and `report` are the sum of the row counts of every statement of the load script (251 orders: 502, then 753 with the DELETE); (3) `run` writes a plan file into `plans/` every time (about 250 KB with its log for two loads); (4) of 53 logged statement pairs in one apply, 33 were the tool's own tracking writes, which are in the tracking tables already; (5) `report` splits one event over four tables with "started" and "completed" rows apart, statements as 12-character hashes, and "by" the database login; (6) `plan` printed the same DDB-302 note several times, a "not interactive" note with no question open, and nine DDB-424 errors for one cause on a fresh project; (7) no structure-only plan.
- **Written**: `docs/research/deploy-and-refresh.md` (superseded in entry 131 by the lifecycle docs): the deploy/refresh matrix, one event model, the verbs, hints for every interface, eight decisions for the owner and an order of work. Draft; nothing built.

## 131. The lifecycle model written down (docs only)

- **Decided with the owner**: nouns `project` (offline) and `connection` (online); actions status, deploy, refresh, monitor; plans are content-addressed and events are separate runs; refresh plans are compiled and committable; project settings (`plans.*.keep`, `audit`) set plan storage and audit level; refresh checks `none|project|objects|live`; old verbs removed with no aliases; `help code`, `ui terminal|web|mcp`, `connection inspect`, `project tests run`, `connection tests run`.
- **Written**: `docs/research/lifecycle-model.md` (design, tables for every command and parameter, term and settings lookups), `docs/research/lifecycle-implementation.md` (phases and a status log), `docs/concepts.md` (for users). Removed `docs/research/deploy-and-refresh.md`.
- **Verified**: docs only; nothing built.

## 132. A scheduled `run` no longer fails from its second run of the day (phase 1.1, run lane)

- **Changed**: `run` names its plan `ref-<utc yyyyMMddTHHmmss>-<4 random hex>` (`PlanDocument.CreateRunId`) instead of date + content hash, so the "a plan is applied once" rule (DDB-438) no longer meets a repeated run. `plan` and `apply` keep the content id until the deploy flow is rebuilt (`docs/research/lifecycle-implementation.md`, phase 3).
- **Tests**: a unit test of the id format; the conformance test `Run_only_runs_routine_loads_and_report_shows_what_happened` runs three times in a row and counts three completed `ref-` rows.
- **Verified**: unit suite (1818) and that conformance test on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17. Fabric not run.

## 133. The rows of a load are the rows inserted (phase 1.2)

- **Changed**: the executor (`MutationGate`) reports the count of the last statement that reports one instead of the driver's total over the script, so a load of 251 rows records 251, not 502. No rendered file changes. A hook script's count is likewise its last counting statement's.
- **Tests**: `Run_only_runs_routine_loads_and_report_shows_what_happened` asserts the recorded counts (one load of 3 rows, three of 4). It fails with the change reverted.
- **Verified**: unit suite and that test on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17. Fabric not run.

## 134. Less noise from `plan` (phase 1.3)

- **Changed**: when more than one rendered file is missing or differs, `plan` gives one DDB-424 (the count, the first three files, `render --write`) instead of one per file (`render --check` still lists every file). The "not interactive and no --answers file" note is no longer printed up front; it is added to the "N question(s) are open" refusal, where it explains something.
- **Tests**: the conformance test `The_whole_loop_works_and_every_refusal_holds` asserts one `error DDB-424`.
- **Verified**: unit suite; the `Group=apply` conformance tests on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 (the one test that needed the new assertion fixed was re-run). Fabric not run.

## 135. The command tree: `project`, `connection`, `ui`, `help` (phases 2 and 3 of the lifecycle plan)

- **Changed (breaking, no aliases)**: 29 verbs became 25 commands under four nouns. `new` → `project create`; `define` → `project model update`; `validate` + `render` → `project compile` (validates, then writes `rendered/`; `--check` for CI; `--content` shows the files without writing); `test` → `project tests run`; `sample`, `seed`, `import`, `agent-kit` → `project …`; `loads`, `graph`, `metadata`, `review` → `project show loads|graph|metadata|plan`; `init`, `publish-metadata`, `load-seeds` → `connection init|publish|seed`; `check` → `connection status`; `report` → `connection monitor`; `diff` → `connection compare`; `run` → `connection refresh`; `plan` + `apply` + `ack` → `connection deploy` (plan, show, ask, apply; `--write-plan`; `--apply-plan <file>`; `--ack kind:name --reason`; `--yes`); `tui`, `web`, `mcp` → `ui terminal|web|mcp`; `explain`, `matrix` → `help code|matrix`.
- **`--resume` is gone**: applying a plan that stopped part-way continues it (the finished steps are skipped, after the same check that the live objects are where the finished steps left them); a completed plan is still refused (DDB-438). A plan that fails again at the same step now leaves one more `started`/`failed` pair in `migration_log`.
- **Specs and documents**: `CommandSpec` has the whole path, a lane and a disposition code (`P→`, `P⇒S`, …); `docs/commands.md` shows the disposition; the JSON `command` field is the path; `schemas/output.schema.json` was rewritten for the new names (`project compile` carries the data of both halves; `connection deploy` is one of the plan, apply or ack documents).
- **Interfaces**: MCP tool names are the path with underscores (`connection_deploy`); a read-only server offers `connection_deploy` to plan, without `apply_plan`, `yes` and `ack`; `.mcp.json` and the MCPB manifest start `ui mcp`. The web page runs `project_compile` held to `--check`, and `connection_deploy` with `write_plan`. The terminal menu is grouped Look at the project / Define / Deploy and refresh / Record and report, and plans from it stop at the plan (applying is the plan window).
- **Also**: the rendered files' header says `Generated by \`project compile\`` (the goldens changed by that line only); the hints in diagnostics, the skill, the MCP prompts, the README, the guides and the template READMEs use the new names. DESIGN.md had its command names swept but its prose around them is not rewritten (phase 9). The tests were moved by script; the rendering tests call the rendering half of compile directly (`TestSupport.RenderFiles`) because their fixtures do not pass the project's other checks.
- **Not done yet**: the positional connection name (`--connection` stays), `connection inspect`, `project model create`, `project tests list`, `connection tests run`; the plan policy, events and refresh checks (phases 4 to 6).
- **Verified**: unit suite (1817) and every conformance group (apply, quick, templates, release) on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17. The terminal screens were not driven (`scripts/tui_drive.py`); Fabric not run.

## 136. Every header says what a command reads and writes; commands say what comes next (phase 7, first part)

- **Changed**: each command's header line now carries `reads/writes: P⇒S` (the code of `docs/concepts.md`) and, for inspect, deploy and refresh, `lane: …`; `connection deploy` shows the code of the path it took (`C→P` for `--write-plan`, `P⇒T` for `--ack`). A command ends with a `Next:` block of the commands that follow from what happened (`project compile` → `project tests run`, `connection status`; `connection status` → `connection deploy` or `connection refresh`; a plan → `connection deploy --apply-plan <file>`; a stopped apply → the same command again; `connection init` → `connection deploy`; …), and the JSON document has a `next` array of them (`schemas/output.schema.json`).
- **Tests**: `A_command_says_what_comes_next…` and `Every_header_says_what_the_command_reads_and_writes_and_its_lane` (JsonOutputTests).
- **Not done**: the hints in the terminal menu and the web tabs (phase 8), one error per cause outside `plan`, the traffic-light lines of `connection status` (phase 6).
- **Verified**: unit suite. The conformance groups were not re-run for this change (only text was added to the end of outputs).

## 137. The lifecycle settings are read (phase 5, first part: settings only)

- **Added**: `plans:` (`deploy`/`refresh`: `keep` committed|database|ephemeral, `audit` minimal|standard|full; `deploy.require_clean_tree`), `refresh:` (`check` none|project|objects|live, `on_fail` block|warn) and `retention.statement_logs_days`, at the project and (`plans`, `refresh`) per connection. `ProjectConfig.Lifecycle` / `LifecycleOf(connection)` give what is in force; the header's `Effective:` line names it when it is not the default. `schemas/config.schema.json` has them; `ProjectConfigTests` checks the schema and the loader agree on each case.
- **Not acting yet**: nothing reads the settings. They are acted on by the compiled refresh plan, the refresh checks and the plan policy (phases 4 and 5 of `docs/research/lifecycle-implementation.md`).
- **Verified**: unit suite.

## 138. Refresh runs from a compiled plan; tracking layout 5 (phases 4 and 5 of the lifecycle plan)

- **The compiled plan**: `project compile` writes `rendered/<connection>/refresh.plan.yml` for each connection (`RefreshPlanBuilder`, `RefreshPlanDocument`): the routine loads in dependency order (each model's default operation, with the load script and resolver by path and hash, the values the project's files give, the watermark's `on_null`/`initial`, and the safe data hooks around it), `requires` (the shape the declared columns give each loaded table, as the engine reports it), `project_hash` (a hash over the expected structure of the whole connection: each table model's shape, each view's definition) and `excluded` (models with loads that are not routine, each with the reason: a copy, a load that needs a value from a person, a hook that is not a safe data hook). The file carries a hash of its own content like a deploy plan, is deterministic, and is checked by `project compile --check` like any rendered file. A change of a parameter value changes it (and no load script).
- **`connection refresh`** (`RefreshCommand`, replacing `RunCommand`): reads that plan and the scripts it names (each must have the recorded hash, DDB-448; a missing plan is DDB-446), applies `refresh.check` or `--check` (**none**: nothing; **project**: the project's `project_hash` against the last completed deploy's; **objects**, the default: each loaded object against the latest recorded shape, tracking tables only; **live**: against the catalog, and a change made outside the tool is named), stops on a failure before anything runs (DDB-447) or runs anyway with `--on-fail warn`, finds the watermarks with the resolvers, and runs the loads through `ApplyEngine` in refresh mode: no plan is verified against the live objects, no catalog read per load unless the check is `live`, nothing to continue, an event of its own (`ref-<utc>-<4 hex>`) in `migration_log` with `lane = 'refresh'` and the compiled plan's hash; the plan text is kept only when `plans.refresh.audit` is `full`. It writes no plan file. The test gate and the recording of native definitions and metadata belong to deploy and are not run by a refresh.
- **Layout 5**: `migration_log` has `lane` and `project_hash`; a deploy records its lane and the project hash its plan carried (`Plan.ProjectHash`, in the plan file and `schemas/plan.schema.json`). The init script never alters, so `init --apply` on a layout 4 store does not claim it (the version row is written only when the newest column is there) and the tool keeps saying `init --upgrade`; the upgrade adds the two columns. `StatusAsync` reads the version table directly instead of asking the catalog whether it exists, so the readiness check is no metadata query.
- **`connection monitor`**: a failed refresh is something to attend to only until a later refresh completes; the message for a deploy that did not complete says to apply the same plan again.
- **Defaults**: `plans.deploy.require_clean_tree` is true by default (the guard that was there stays) and applies to a refresh as well.
- **Tests**: `RefreshPlanTests` (the builder, determinism, tampering and damage), `RefreshTests` (the command up to the point where a connection is needed), `ProjectConfigTests` (the new settings), `TrackingUpgradeConformanceTests` (4 to 5 on both engines), and the conformance test `Refresh_runs_the_compiled_routine_loads_under_the_check_chosen_and_each_run_is_an_event` (every check against a real deploy, a model change, a change made outside the tool, the engine's own error with `--check none`, and a spy on the read session proving `--check none` issues no catalog query).
- **Not done**: `plans.*.keep` database and ephemeral (and the `plan_store` table), the deploy event as its own id, `audit` for a deploy, statement-log retention, `connection status` and `monitor` as the traffic-light views, `refresh` of copies.
- **Verified**: unit suite and the conformance groups on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17 (see the next entry for the full run). Fabric not run.

## 139. `connection inspect`, `project tests list`, events in `monitor`, the landing lines of `status` (phases 6 and "later" of the lifecycle plan)

- **`connection inspect`**: for each login (read, write) whether its variable is set, whether it connects and as whom, the server's version line, and (write) whether it may create tables in the database, all asked with SELECTs through a read session opened on either login (`ReadSession.OpenForInspectionAsync`; the read guard still applies, nothing is created); whether the tracking tables are ready (or missing, or of another layout); which schema names the models use and whether they exist. A failure is the driver's type and error number only; the password is never printed. Exit 1 when something is in the way, with a `Next:` for it (`connection init`, `connection init --upgrade`).
- **`project tests list`**: the project's tests with kind, severity, tags, model and number of cases, selectable by `--tag` and `--kind`; nothing runs.
- **`connection monitor`**: the first table is now the events (one row per deploy or refresh run, with its lane and last status), built from `migration_log` by `AuditLog.EventsAsync` (a plan that stopped and continued is one event). The JSON key is `events` (was `applied_plans`).
- **`connection status`** starts with the landing lines: **Structure** (in sync / pending, counting the structure steps a deploy would run apart from the routine loads it would also run / needs attention), **Deploy** and **Refresh** (the newest of each: id, state, time and how long ago), **Policy** (the lifecycle settings in force). The JSON has them as `summary`.
- **Not built**: `project model create`, `connection tests run` (its meaning was never settled), the positional connection name.
- **Verified**: unit suite and the conformance groups on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17, including a conformance test of `inspect` before and after init and a deploy, and the status lines after a deploy and refreshes. Fabric not run.

## 140. The plan policy acts: ephemeral plans, audit levels, statement-log retention; lanes in the interfaces (phases 5, 7 and 8)

- **`plans.deploy.keep: ephemeral`**: planning writes the files under `.dbdatabuild/plans/<connection>/` (git ignores that folder; `--output` still wins) instead of `plans/`, and applying a plan from there removes the files once it succeeded. **`keep: database`** is refused with a message that says it is not built yet.
- **`plans.<lane>.audit`**: `full` keeps the text of the plan in the event and the text of each DDL statement; `standard` keeps the hashes, who and the commit but no text; `minimal` keeps the event and its steps without who or the commit. Below `full` the decisions embedded in applied plans are not stored, so the column-history report has nothing to read (said in the settings table of `docs/concepts.md`).
- **`retention.statement_logs_days`**: the next deploy or refresh removes statement logs last written more than that many days ago (0 keeps them) and says so; `StatementLogRetention`. Log file names no longer contain a space (`connection deploy` was in them).
- **Interfaces**: the MCP tool descriptions start with `[lane · code]`, the terminal catalog carries lane and code (`CommandInfo.Lane`, `Disposition`), and the web page's screens show their lane next to the name with what they touch in the tooltip.
- **Verified**: unit suite (1847) and the conformance test `The_project_settings_say_what_a_deploy_keeps_and_records_and_how_long_the_logs_stay` on both engines; the full conformance run before this change (187 passed, 5 skipped by design) is on the same tree otherwise. Fabric not run.

## 141. Plans kept in the database; `project model create`; the terminal menu (phases 5 and "later")

- **`plans.deploy.keep: database`**: tracking layout 5 has a `plan_store` table (connection, plan id, plan hash, plan text, who, when). `connection deploy --write-plan` under that setting writes no file: it keeps the plan in the table (the tracking connection's write login is needed, and the tool says so when it is not set) and prints the id; `connection deploy --apply-plan <id>` reads it back by id (a file of that name wins if there is one), checks the plan's own hash (a plan edited in the table is DDB-435), and applies it like any other: once (DDB-438), with the allowances, continuing if it stopped. `--connection` names which connection's store when the project has several.
- **`project model create <schema_name.object_name>`**: a definition and a placeholder query that runs, in the project's layout (folder or dotted), for a view, a full table, or an incremental model by key or by time range; `--connection` names the connections. It refuses a name that is taken or not a name, and a kind it cannot scaffold (copy, native, mapped).
- **Terminal menu** lists the new commands (`project model create`, `project tests list`, `connection inspect`, `connection compare`); the form of a command takes the values an option accepts (`--check`, `--on-fail`, `--kind`) from the command definition.
- **Verified**: unit suite (1853); the conformance tests of the settings (ephemeral, audit, retention, database: store, tamper, apply by id, applied once) and of the tracking upgrades, on SQL Server 2022 and PostgreSQL 17; the terminal's first screen driven with `scripts/tui_drive.py` (pyte from a downloaded wheel). The full conformance run before these two changes was green (189 passed, 5 skipped by design).

## 142. A walk through the new cycle on SQL Server; `monitor` can be narrowed

- **Method**: the `starter` project on SQL Server 2022 from an empty database, by hand: `connection inspect`, `init`, `seed`, `project compile`, `connection deploy --yes`, `status`, `refresh`, `monitor`, then a new column in a model: `compile`, `refresh` (refused by the objects check with the remedy), `deploy` (stopped at the question about the new column's history; answered with `--answers`), `refresh`, `status`.
- **Found and fixed**: `connection deploy` and `connection refresh` printed the command header twice (the plan's and the apply's) and the interactive deploy named the plan twice; `connection seed` suggested `init --apply` after init was done; `inspect` columns did not line up; the closing line of a refresh said "Applied plan ref-…".
- **Added**: `connection monitor --lane deploy|refresh` (the events and their DDL and loads of one lane), `--event <id>` (every state of one event, its DDL and its loads; `data.event` in the document), `--attention` (only what needs attention in the text).
- **Verified**: unit suite and the conformance test of the refresh (which now also exercises the three options) on both engines.

## 143. Sample and template work written down; release 0.4.0

- **Checked**: no template has project macros or a copy; only `starter` was walked through the new cycle. The remaining work is OPEN-ITEMS section S (settings, walk-throughs, fan-in and fan-out samples, a refresh plan with `excluded` copies, dynamic-macro examples).
- **Release 0.4.0**: `<Version>` and the Scoop manifest moved to 0.4.0; the release workflow's smoke test uses `help matrix` (the old `matrix` verb is gone). The Scoop hash is filled in by the `scoop` job of the release workflow once the artifact exists, not by hand.

## 144. OPEN-ITEMS section S: two new templates (`stores`, `macros`), lifecycle settings and walk-throughs in the others, and what walking them found

- **Added**: `templates/stores` (fan-in copy with a slice from two PostgreSQL shop systems into a SQL Server warehouse and a PostgreSQL reporting database, a model built on both engines, central tracking, per-connection string semantics, refresh plans that list the copy under `excluded`) and `templates/macros` (scalar and table macros, a default parameter, a type alias, one macro for a live table and its history, `NAME` parameters; README note that a macro is never run by an engine). The commented `plans:`/`refresh:`/`retention:` block is in `retail`, `chinook` and `adventureworks`. Every README has the walk-through of the cycle (`inspect`, `seed`, `init`, `status`, `deploy`, `refresh`, `monitor`, the four `refresh.check` levels in `retail`'s), with output from real runs only. `stores` is a new template and not an addition to `retail` because `retail` is one connection with a SQL Server profile and every test over it assumes that.
- **Found by walking the templates, and fixed** (`docs/research/template-findings.md`, M1 to M7):
  - a cast to or from a DuckDB `ENUM` (a macro that uses a `CREATE TYPE ... AS ENUM`) reached the rendered script as `CAST(x AS ENUM)` with only a DDB-305 warning; the lowerer now refuses it (DDB-324). A type alias (`CREATE TYPE money AS DECIMAL(12, 2)`) lowers to the type it stands for;
  - a DATE that DuckDB folds while it expands a macro (a default parameter) was written as `CAST(19904 AS DATE)`, which DuckDB and PostgreSQL refuse and SQL Server reads as 1954; it is now `DATE '2024-06-30'`;
  - `project model update --check` failed (DDB-108) in any project with a copy, and (DDB-219) for a query given a table by a `NAME` parameter; both fixed;
